Advaita Treatises

Naiṣkarmyasiddhi

By Sureśvara

Info

Complete local Sanskrit text of all four adhyāyas; source paragraphs are grouped by chapter and rendered in Devanāgarī and IAST. Original English translation is provided for every text unit. Texts 1.2–1.4 also have literal readings and word-for-word glosses; the remaining units do not yet have separate glosses.

Text source: Sanskrit Wikisource text compiled and transliterated by the ERC-funded Intellexus project.

Adhyāya 1

Chapter heading

Adhyāya 1

अथ प्रथमोऽध्यायः ।

Now begins the first chapter.

Text 2

1 · 2

आब्रह्मस्तम्बपर्यन्तैः सर्वप्राणिभिः सर्वप्रकारस्यापि दुःखस्य स्वरसत एव जिहासितत्वात्तन्निवृत्त्यर्था प्रवृत्तिरस्ति स्वरसत एव ।

Literal translation

All living beings, from Brahmā to a clump of grass, naturally wish to abandon suffering of every kind. Their action is naturally for its cessation.

Word-for-word
आ-ब्रह्म-स्तम्ब-पर्यन्तैः from Brahmā to a clump of grass
सर्व-प्राणिभिः by all living beings
सर्व-प्रकारस्य of every kind
अपि even
दुःखस्य of suffering
स्वरसतः naturally
एव indeed
जिहासितत्वात् because of wishing to abandon
तत्-निवृत्ति-अर्था for its cessation
प्रवृत्तिः action
अस्ति exists
स्वरसतः naturally
एव indeed
Expanded source translation

All beings, from Brahmā down to a clump of grass, naturally wish to be rid of suffering of every kind; therefore they naturally act for its cessation.

Text 3

1 · 3

दुःखस्य च देहोपादानैकहेतुत्वाद्देहस्य च पूर्वोपचितधर्माधर्ममूलत्वादनुच्छित्तिः ।

Literal translation

Suffering does not cease: taking a body is its sole cause, and the body is rooted in merit and demerit accumulated before.

Word-for-word
दुःखस्य of suffering
च and
देह-उपादान-एक-हेतुत्वात् because taking a body is the sole cause
देहस्य of the body
च and
पूर्व-उपचित previously accumulated
धर्म-अधर्म-मूलत्वात् because rooted in merit and demerit
अनुच्छित्तिः no cessation
Expanded source translation

Suffering has the body as its sole basis, and the body is rooted in merit and demerit accumulated previously; therefore suffering does not cease.

Text 4

1 · 4

तयोश्चविहितप्रतिषिद्धकर्ममूलत्वादनिवृत्तिः ।

Literal translation

They do not cease because those two [merit and demerit] are rooted in prescribed and forbidden actions.

Word-for-word
तयोः of those two
च and
विहित prescribed
प्रतिषिद्ध forbidden
कर्म action
मूलत्वात् because of being rooted
अनिवृत्तिः no cessation
Expanded source translation

Those two are rooted in prescribed and prohibited action, and so they do not cease.

Text 5

1 · 5

कर्मणश्च रागद्वेषास्पदत्वाद्रागद्वेषयोश्च शोभनाशोभनाध्यासनिबन्धनत्वादध्यासस्य चाविचारितसिद्धद्वैतवस्तुनिमित्तत्वाद्द्वैतस्य च शुक्तिकारजतादिवत्सर्वस्यापि स्वतस्सिद्धाद्वितीयात्मानवबोधमात्रोपादानत्वादव्यावृत्तिः ।

Action rests on attachment and aversion; these arise from imagining things to be desirable or undesirable. That superimposition is grounded in an unexamined assumption of duality. All duality persists because the naturally evident, non-dual Self is not known—just as silver is imagined in a shell.

Text 6

1 · 6

अतः सर्वानर्थहेतुरात्मानवबोध एव । सुखस्य चानागमापायिनोऽपरतन्त्रस्यात्मस्वभावत्वात्तस्यानवबोधः पिधानम् । अतस्तस्यात्यन्तोच्छित्तावशेषपुरुषार्थपरिसमाप्तिः । अज्ञाननिवृत्तेश्च सम्यग्ज्ञानस्वरूपलाभमात्रहेतुत्वात्तदुपादानम् । अशेषानर्थहेत्वात्मानवबोधविषयस्य चानागमिकप्रत्यक्षादिलौकिकप्रमाणाविषयत्वाद्वेदान्तागमवाक्यादेव सम्यग्ज्ञानम् ।

Therefore ignorance of the Self alone is the cause of every ill. Happiness, which neither comes nor goes and depends on nothing else, is the Self’s own nature; ignorance veils it. When ignorance is wholly destroyed, every human aim is fulfilled. Ignorance is removed only by the attainment of right knowledge. Since the Self, the cause of every ill, is not an object of ordinary means of knowledge such as perception, right knowledge arises only from the words of the Vedānta.

Text 7

1 · 7

अतोऽशेषवेदान्तसारसंग्रहप्रकरणमिदमारभ्यते ।

Therefore this treatise, which gathers the essence of all Vedānta, is begun.

Text 8

1 · 8

तत्राभिलषितार्थप्रचयाय प्रकरणार्थसंसूत्रणाय चायमाद्यः श्लोकः ।

To set out the desired end and state the subject of the treatise in brief, this is the opening verse.

Text 9

1 · 9

खानिलाग्न्यब्धरित्र्यन्तं स्रक्फणीवोद्गतं यतः ।

From whom space, wind, fire, water, and earth arise, like a garland mistaken for a snake—

Verse 1.1

1.1

ध्वान्तच्छिदे नमस्तस्मै हरये बुद्धिसाक्षिणे ॥ १॥

Salutation to that Hari, the witness of the intellect, who destroys darkness.

Text 11

1 · 11

स्वसम्प्रदायस्य चोदितप्रमाणपूर्वकत्वज्ञापनाय विशिष्टगुणसम्बन्धसंकीर्तनपूर्विका गुरोर्नमस्कारक्रिया ।

The teacher is saluted after his distinctive qualities are recounted, to show that the authority of our own tradition rests on the authority already taught.

Text 12

1 · 12

अलब्ध्वातिशयं यस्माद्व्यावृत्तास्तमबादयः ।

From whom water and the other elements recede, having found no one superior—

Verse 1.2

1.2

गरीयसे नमस्तस्मा अविद्याग्रन्थिभेदिने ॥ २॥

Salutation to that greater one, who breaks the knot of ignorance.

Text 14

1 · 14

नमस्कारनिमित्तस्वाशयाविष्करणार्थः ।

This verse discloses the intention that prompted the salutation.

Text 15

1 · 15

वेदान्तोदरसंगूढं संसारोत्सारि वस्तुगम् ।

The reality concealed in the heart of the Vedānta, which carries one beyond saṃsāra,

Verse 1.3

1.3

ज्ञानं व्याकृतमप्यन्यैर्वक्ष्ये गुर्वनुशिक्षया ॥ ३॥

though explained by others, I shall expound under my teacher’s instruction.

Text 17

1 · 17

किंविशयं प्रकरणमिति चेत्तदुपन्यासः ।

If it is asked, “What is the subject of this treatise?”, it is now stated:

Text 18

1 · 18

यत्सिद्धाविदमः सिद्धिर्यदसिद्धौ न किंचन ।

That whose realization makes this [world] realized, and whose non-realization leaves nothing [truly known],

Verse 1.4

1.4

प्रत्यग्धर्मैकनिष्ठस्य याथात्म्यं वक्ष्यते स्फुटम् ॥ ४॥

the true nature of the inward Self, whose reality is its own essential nature, will be clearly set forth.

Text 20

1 · 20

विवक्षितप्रकरणार्थप्ररोचनायानुक्तदुरुक्ताप्रामाण्यकारणशङ्काव्युदासेनस्वगुरोः प्रामाण्योपवर्णनम् ।

To encourage attention to the intended subject, the authority of the teacher is described after removing doubts that he is unauthoritative because of omissions, errors, or other defects.

Text 21

1 · 21

गुरूक्तो वेदराद्धान्तस्तत्र नो वच्म्यशक्तितः ।

“I am unable to repeat the Veda’s established teaching as spoken by my teacher.

Verse 1.5

1.5

सहस्रकिरणव्याप्ते खद्योतः किं प्रकाशयेत् ॥ ५॥

What could a firefly illumine when the thousand-rayed sun shines everywhere?”

Text 23

1 · 23

गुरुणैव वेदार्थस्य परिसमापितत्वात्प्रकरणोक्तौ ख्यात्याद्यप्रामाण्यकारणाशङ्केति चेत्तद्व्युदासार्थमाह ।

If one objects, “Since the teacher has already completed the exposition of the Veda’s meaning, a treatise by you may be suspected of being unauthoritative, prompted by fame and the like,” he replies to dispel that doubt:

Text 24

1 · 24

न ख्यातिलाभपूजार्थं ग्रन्थोऽस्माभिरुदीर्यते ।

We do not compose this treatise for fame, gain, or honor.

Verse 1.6

1.6

स्वबोधपरिशुद्ध्यर्थं ब्रह्मविन्निकषाश्मसु ॥ ६॥

It is for the purification of my own understanding, as a touchstone is used by those who know Brahman.

Text 26

1 · 26

अनर्थानर्थहेतुपुरुषार्थतद्धेतुप्रकरणार्थसंग्रहज्ञापनायोपन्यासः । ऐकात्म्याप्रतिपत्तिर्या स्वात्मानिभवसंश्रया ।

The subject is introduced to state the subject of the treatise, the human end, and its cause: failure to realize one’s own oneness, which is grounded in the experience of oneself,

Verse 1.7

1.7

साऽविद्या संसृतेर्बीजं तन्नाशो मुक्तिरात्मनः ॥ ७॥

is ignorance, the seed of saṃsāra; its destruction is liberation for the Self.

Text 28

1 · 28

पुरुषार्थहेतोरवशिष्टत्वात्तदभिव्याहारः ।

Since the human end remains to be stated, it is now expressed.

Text 29

1 · 29

वेदावसानवाक्योत्थसम्यग्ज्ञानाशुशुक्षणिः ।

The fire of right knowledge, arising from the final words of the Veda,

Verse 1.8

1.8

दन्दहीत्यात्मनो मोहं न कर्माप्रतिकूलतः ॥ ८॥

burns away delusion about the Self; action does not oppose it.

Text 31

1 · 31

प्रतिज्ञातार्थसंशुद्ध्यर्थं पूर्वपक्षोक्तिः ।

An opponent’s position is stated in order to clarify the thesis.

Text 32

1 · 32

तत्र ज्ञानमभ्युपगम्य तावदुपन्यासः ।

First, the argument proceeds while provisionally admitting knowledge.

Text 33

1 · 33

मुक्तेः क्रियाभिः सिद्धत्वाज्ज्ञानं तत्र करोति किम् ।

If liberation is accomplished through actions, what work is left for knowledge?

Verse 1.9

1.9

कथं चेच्छृणु तत्सर्वं प्रणिधाय मनो यथा ॥ ९॥

If you ask how, listen to the whole account with a steady mind.

Text 35

1 · 35

अकुर्वतः क्रियाः काम्या निषिद्धास्त्यजतस्तथा ।

One who refrains from desire-motivated actions and abandons prohibited ones,

Verse 1.10

1.10

नित्यनैमित्तिकं कर्म विधिवच्चानुतिष्ठतः ॥ १०॥

yet duly performs the obligatory and occasional rites,

Text 37

1 · 37

किमतो भवति ।

what follows from that?

Text 38

1 · 38

काम्यकर्मफलं तस्माद्देवादीमं न ढौकते ।

The fruits of desire-motivated action do not reach him, even those leading to the world of the gods.

Verse 1.11

1.11

निषिद्धस्य निरस्तत्वान्नारकीं नैत्यधोजनिम् ॥ ११॥

Because prohibited action has been removed, he does not fall into a low birth in hell.

Text 40

1 · 40

देहारम्भकयोश्च धर्माधर्मयोर्ज्ञानिना सह कर्मिणः समानौ चोद्यपरिहारौ ।

The objection and its answer concerning merit and demerit—which cause the body—are the same for the man of knowledge and for the ritualist.

Text 41

1 · 41

वर्तमानमिदं याभ्यां शरीरं सुखदुःखदम् ।

These two have produced this present body, which gives pleasure and pain.

Verse 1.12

1.12

आरब्धं पुण्यपापाभ्यां भोगादेव तयोः क्षयः ॥ १२॥

Their exhaustion comes only through being experienced.

Text 43

1 · 43

काम्यप्रतिषिद्धकर्मफलत्वात्संसारस्य तन्निरासेनैवाशेषानर्थनिरासस्य सिद्धत्वात्किं नित्यानुष्ठानेनेति चेत्तन्न । तदकरणादप्यनर्थप्रसक्तेः ।

If one objects, “Since saṃsāra results from desire-driven and forbidden actions, and removing those alone removes every ill, why perform obligatory rites?”, that is wrong: omitting them also leads to harm.

Text 44

1 · 44

नित्यानुष्ठानतश्चैनं प्रत्यवायो न संस्पृशेत् ।

By performing obligatory duties, no adverse consequence will touch him.

Verse 1.13

1.13

अनादृत्यात्मविज्ञानमतः कर्माणि संश्रयेत् ॥ १३॥

Therefore he should rely on actions, disregarding knowledge of the Self.

Text 46

1 · 46

अभ्युपेत्यैवमुच्यते न तु यथावस्थितात्मवस्तुविषयं ज्ञानमस्ति । तत्प्रतिपादकप्रमाणाभावात् ।

This is said only for the sake of argument; in truth, there is no knowledge of the Self as it really is, because there is no means of knowledge that teaches it.

Text 47

1 · 47

यावन्त्यश्चेह विद्यन्ते श्रुतयस्स्मृतिभिस्सह ।

All the Vedic and traditional texts that are found here

Verse 1.14

1.14

विदधत्युरुयत्नेन कर्मातो भूरिसाधनम् ॥ १४॥

prescribe action with great insistence; therefore action has many means in its favor.

Text 49

1 · 49

स्यात्प्रमाणसम्भवो भवदपराधादिति चेत्तन्न । यतः ।

If one says, “Knowledge could be authoritative; the fault may lie with you,” that is not so, because:

Text 50

1 · 50

यत्नतो वीक्षमाणोऽपि विधिं ज्ञानस्य न क्वचित् ।

Even when I search carefully, I find no injunction to knowledge anywhere

Verse 1.15

1.15

श्रुतौ स्मृतौ वा पश्यामि विश्वासो नान्यतोऽस्ति नः ॥ १५॥

in the Veda or the traditional texts; we trust nothing else.

Text 52

1 · 52

स्यात्प्रवृत्तिरन्तरेणापि विधिं लोकवदिति चेत्तन्न । यतः ।

If one says that action can arise without an injunction, as it does in ordinary life, that is not so, because:

Text 53

1 · 53

अन्तरेण विधिं मोहाद्यः कुर्यात्साम्परायिकम् ।

Whoever performs an otherworldly rite through delusion, without an injunction,

Verse 1.16

1.16

न तत्स्यादुपकाराय भस्मनीव हुतं हविः ॥ १६॥

gains no benefit from it, like an oblation poured into ashes.

Text 55

1 · 55

अभ्युपगतप्रामाण्यवेदार्थविज्जैमिन्यनुशासनाच्च ।

This also follows from the teaching of Jaimini, who accepts the authority of the Veda.

Text 56

1 · 56

"आम्नायस्य क्रियार्थत्वादानर्थक्यम् " इतोऽन्यथा ।

“Since the Veda is concerned with action, otherwise it would be purposeless”—

Verse 1.17

1.17

इति साटोपमाहोच्चैर्वेदविज्जैमिनिः स्वयम् ॥ १७॥

thus Jaimini, the knower of the Veda, himself declared emphatically.

Text 58

1 · 58

मन्त्रवर्णाच्च ।

And from the wording of the mantra:

Text 59

1 · 59

"कुर्वन्नेवेह कर्माणि जिजीविषेच्छतं समाः " ।

“By performing actions here alone, one should wish to live a hundred years.”

Verse 1.18

1.18

इति मन्त्रोऽपि निश्शेषं कर्मण्यायुरवासृजत् ॥ १८॥

This mantra, too, assigns one’s entire life to action.

Text 61

1 · 61

ज्ञानिनश्च वस्तुनि वाक्यप्रामाण्याभ्युपगमाद्वाक्यस्य च क्रियापदप्रधानत्वात्ततश्चाभिप्रेतज्ञानाभावः ।

Since the knower accepts the authority of sentences about existing things, while sentences are chiefly governed by action-words, the knowledge intended here does not arise.

Text 62

1 · 62

विरहय्य क्रियां नैव संहन्यन्ते पदान्यपि ।

Even without an action, words do not combine into a sentence;

Verse 1.19

1.19

न समस्त्यपदं वाक्यं यत्स्याज्ज्ञानविधायकम् ॥ १९॥

there is no sentence lacking a verb that could prescribe knowledge.

Text 64

1 · 64

ज्ञानाभ्युपगमेऽपि न दोषः । यतः ।

Even if knowledge is admitted, there is no defect, because:

Text 65

1 · 65

कर्मणोऽङ्गाङ्गिभावेन स्वप्रधानतयाऽथवा ।

its relation to action, either as an auxiliary or as the principal element,

Verse 1.20

1.20

सम्बन्धस्येह संसिद्धेर्ज्ञाने सत्यप्यदोषतः ॥ २०॥

is firmly established here; therefore there is no defect even if knowledge exists.

Text 67

1 · 67

यस्माज्ज्ञानाभ्युपगमानभ्युपगमेऽपि न ज्ञानान्मुक्तिः ।

For, whether knowledge is admitted or not, liberation does not arise from knowledge.

Text 68

1 · 68

अतः सर्वाश्रमाणां हि वाङ्मनःकायकर्मभिः ।

Therefore, for people in every stage of life, through the actions of speech, mind, and body,

Verse 1.21

1.21

स्वनुष्ठितैर्यथाशक्ति मुक्तिः स्यान्नान्यसाधनात् ॥ २१॥

duly performed to the best of their ability, liberation may be attained; no other means is needed.

Text 70

1 · 70

असदर्थप्रलापोऽयमिति दूषणसंभावनायाह ।

He says this to show that the claim is only foolish talk:

Text 71

1 · 71

इति हृष्टधियां वाचः स्वप्रज्ञाऽऽध्मातचेतसाम् ।

“The words of those whose minds delight in themselves, whose hearts are inflated by their own intelligence,

Verse 1.22

1.22

घुष्यन्ते यज्ञशालासु धूमानद्धधियां किल ॥ २२॥

are proclaimed in sacrificial halls by minds indeed clouded by smoke.”

Text 73

1 · 73

दूषणोपक्रमावधिज्ञापनायाह ।

He now indicates the scope of the refutation:

Text 74

1 · 74

अत्राभिदध्महे दोषान् क्रमशो न्यायबृंहितैः ।

Here we shall point out the defects one by one, with arguments developed by reasoning,

Verse 1.23

1.23

वचोभिः पूर्वपक्षोक्तिघातिभिर्नातिसंभ्रमात् ॥ २३॥

in words that strike down the foregoing position, without undue confusion.

Text 76

1 · 76

चतुर्विधस्यापि कर्मकार्यस्य मुक्तावसंभवान्न मुक्तेः कर्मकार्यत्वम् ।

Liberation cannot be produced by action, since none of the four kinds of products of action can constitute liberation.

Text 77

1 · 77

अज्ञानहानमात्रत्वान्मुक्तेः कर्म न साधनम् ।

Because liberation is only the removal of ignorance, action is not its means.

Verse 1.24

1.24

कर्मापमार्ष्टि नाज्ञानं तमसीवोत्थितं तमः ॥ २४॥

Action does not remove ignorance, just as darkness cannot remove darkness that has arisen.

Text 79

1 · 79

कर्मकार्यत्वाभ्युपगमेऽपि दोष एव ।

Even if liberation were accepted as the product of action, there would still be a defect.

Text 80

1 · 80

एकेन वा भवेन्मुक्तिर्यदि वा सर्वकर्मभिः ।

Liberation would then come either from one action or from all actions.

Verse 1.25

1.25

प्रत्येकं चेद्वृथान्यानि सर्वेभ्योऽप्येककर्मता ॥ २५॥

If each act were a separate means, the others would be useless; or else every act would become a single means for all.

Text 82

1 · 82

सर्वप्रकारस्यापि कर्मण उत्पत्तित एव विशिष्टसाध्याभिसंबन्धान्न परिशेष्यन्यायसिद्धिः ।

Every action, of every kind, is connected from its very origin with a particular result to be accomplished; therefore it cannot be made to serve an unrelated end by the principle of residue.

Text 83

1 · 83

दुरितक्षपणार्थत्वान्न नित्यं स्याद्विमुक्तये ।

An obligatory rite does not lead to liberation merely because it destroys sin;

Verse 1.26

1.26

स्वर्गादिफलसंबन्धात्काम्यं कर्म तथैव न ॥ २६॥

nor does a desire-driven rite do so, since it is connected with results such as heaven.

Text 85

1 · 85

प्रमाणासंभवाच्च ।

And there is no valid means of knowledge for this claim.

Text 86

1 · 86

साध्यसाधनभावोऽयं वचनात्पारलौकिकः ।

I have never heard from the mouth of revelation

Verse 1.27

1.27

नाश्रौषं मोक्षदं कर्म श्रुतेर्वक्त्रात्कथंचन ॥ २७॥

of any action that grants liberation; the relation between an action and a result beyond this world is known only from scripture.

Text 88

1 · 88

अभ्युपगताभ्युपगमाच्च श्वश्रूनिर्गच्छोक्तिवद्भवतो निष्प्रयोजनः प्रलापः ।

Since you merely repeat what has already been admitted, your speech is pointless—like telling a mother-in-law, “Go out!”

Text 89

1 · 89

निषिद्धकाम्ययोस्त्यागस्त्वयापीष्टो यथा मया ।

You too, just as I do, accept the abandonment of prohibited and desire-driven actions.

Verse 1.28

1.28

नित्यस्याफलवत्त्वाच्च न मोक्षः कर्मसाधनः ॥ २८॥

And since obligatory action bears no fruit, liberation is not accomplished by action.

Text 91

1 · 91

एवं तावत् "मुक्तेः क्रियाभिः सिद्धत्वात् " इति निरस्तोऽयं पक्षः । अथाधुना सर्वकर्मप्रवृत्तिहेतुनिरूपणेन यथावस्थितात्मवस्तुविषयकेवलज्ञानमात्रादेव सकलसंसारानर्थनिवृत्तिरितीमं पक्षं द्रढयितुकाम आह ।

Thus the view that liberation is accomplished through actions has been refuted. Now, wishing to establish that the cessation of every ill of saṃsāra comes solely from knowledge of the Self as it truly is, through examining the causes that prompt all action, he says:

Text 92

1 · 92

इह चेदं परीक्ष्यते । किं यथा प्रतिषिद्धेषु यादृच्छिकेषु च कर्मसु स्वाभाविकस्वाशयोत्थनिमित्तवशादेवेदं हितमिदमहितमिति विशेषान् परिकल्प्य मृगतृष्णिकोदकपिपासुरिव लौकिकप्रमाणप्रसिद्धान्येव साधनान्युपादाय हितप्राप्तयेऽहितनिरासाय च स्वयमेव प्रवर्तते निवर्तते च तथैवादृष्टार्थेषु काम्येषु नित्येषु च कर्मसु किं वान्यदेव तत्र प्रवृत्तिनिवृत्तिनिमित्तमिति । किंचातः । यद्येवं श्रुणु । यदि तावद्यथावस्थितवस्तुसम्यग्ज्ञानं प्रमाणभूतं लौकिकमागमिकं वा प्रवृत्तिनिमित्तमिति निश्चीयते निवृत्तिशास्त्रं च नाभ्युपगम्यते तदा हताः कर्मत्यागिनो भ्रान्तिविज्ञानमात्रावष्टम्भादलौकिकप्रमाणोपात्तकर्मानुष्ठानत्यागित्वाच्च । अथ मृगतृष्णिकोदकपिपासुप्रवृत्तिनिमित्तवदयथावस्तुभ्रान्तिविज्ञानमेव सर्वप्रवृत्तिनिमित्तं तदा वर्द्धामहे वयं हताः स्थ यूयमिति ।

Here this matter is examined: in forbidden and spontaneous actions, does one act or refrain by distinguishing what is beneficial from what is harmful, prompted by one’s natural interests—as a person thirsty for water in a mirage acts on the means known through ordinary experience, seeking good and avoiding harm? Or is some other cause responsible for acting and refraining in rites whose results are unseen, whether desired or obligatory? What follows? Listen. If correct knowledge of things as they are—whether gained by ordinary or scriptural means—is held to prompt action, while the scriptural teaching of renunciation is rejected, then those who abandon action are ruined: they have renounced rites prescribed by a superhuman authority, relying only on a mistaken cognition. But if mistaken cognition of things as they are, like the thirst for water in a mirage, is the cause of all action, then we say: “We are thriving; you are the ones who are lost.”

Text 93

1 · 93

हितं संप्रेप्सतां मोहादहितं च जिहासताम् ।

For those who, through delusion, seek to secure what is good and to abandon what is harmful,

Verse 1.29

1.29

उपायान्प्राप्तिहनार्थान् शास्त्रं भासयतेऽर्कवत् ॥ २९॥

scripture reveals the means to gain and avoid them, like the sun.

Text 95

1 · 95

एवं तावत्प्रत्यक्षानुमानागमप्रमाणावष्टम्भादात्मनो निरतिशयसुखहिताव्यतिरेकसिद्धेरहितस्य च षष्ठगोचरवत्स्वत एवानभिसंबन्धादेवंस्वाभाव्यात्मानवबोधमात्रादेव हितं मे स्यादहितं मे मा भूदिति मिथ्याज्ञानं तूषरशुक्तिकानवबोधोत्थमिथ्याज्ञानवत् प्रवृत्तिनिमित्तमिति निर्धारितम् । शास्त्रं च न पदार्थशक्त्याधानकृदिति । अथैतस्यैवोत्तरत्र प्रपञ्च आरभ्यते ।

Thus it has been established, relying on perception, inference, and scripture, that the Self is inseparable from unsurpassed happiness and good, while what is harmful is unrelated to it of itself, like a sixth sense-object. Because this is the Self’s nature, the mere ignorance of it gives rise to the false thought, “May good come to me; may harm not come to me,” just as ignorance of a shell gives rise to the false perception of silver in a barren place. Scripture does not implant powers in things. This very point will now be developed.

Text 96

1 · 96

न परीप्सां जिहासां वा पुंसः शास्त्रं करोति हि ।

Scripture does not create a person’s desire to gain or avoid something;

Verse 1.30

1.30

निजे एव तु ते यस्मात्पश्वादावपि दर्शनात् ॥ ३०॥

both desires are already present by nature, as is seen even in animals.

Text 98

1 · 98

उक्तं तावदनवबुद्धवस्तुयाथात्म्य एव विधिप्रतिषेशशास्त्रेष्वधिक्रियत इति ।

It has already been said that one who does not understand the true nature of things is eligible for the scriptural injunctions and prohibitions.

Text 99

1 · 99

लिप्सतेऽज्ञानतोऽलब्धं कण्ठे चामीकरं यथा ।

Through ignorance one seeks what one has not obtained, like gold hanging from one’s own neck,

Verse 1.31

1.31

वर्जितं च स्वतो भ्रान्त्या छायायामात्मनो यथा ॥ ३१॥

and, through one’s own delusion, rejects what is already present, as one mistakes one’s shadow for something else.

Text 101

1 · 101

भयान्मोहावनद्धात्मा रक्षः परिजिहीर्षति ।

Bound by delusion and fear, a person tries to escape a demon

Verse 1.32

1.32

पच्चापरिहृतं वस्तु तथालब्धं च लिप्सते ॥ ३२॥

that has not been removed, and longs to obtain what has not been gained.

Text 103

1 · 103

तत्रैतेषु चतुर्षु विषयेषु प्राप्तये परीहाराय च विभज्य न्यायः प्रदर्श्यते ।

The reasoning is now shown with respect to these four cases, divided into what is to be gained and what is to be avoided.

Text 104

1 · 104

प्राप्तव्यपरिहार्येषु ज्ञात्वोपायाञ्छृतेः पृथक् ।

Having separately learned from scripture the means regarding what is to be obtained and what is to be avoided,

Verse 1.33

1.33

कृत्वाथ प्राप्नुयात्प्राप्यं तथानिष्टं जहात्यपि ॥ ३३॥

one acts, obtains what is to be obtained, and abandons what is unwanted.

Text 106

1 · 106

अथावशिष्टयोः स्वभावत एव ।

But in the remaining two cases, things already present by nature—

Text 107

1 · 107

परिहृतावाप्तयोर्बोधाद्धानप्राप्ती न कर्मणा ।

their abandonment or attainment comes through knowledge, not through action,

Verse 1.34

1.34

मोहमात्रान्तरायत्वात्क्रियया ते न सिध्यतः ॥ ३४॥

for action cannot accomplish them; only delusion stands in the way.

Text 109

1 · 109

कस्मात्पुनरात्मवस्तुयाथात्म्यावबोधमात्रादेवाभिलषितनिरतिशयसुखावाप्तिनिश्शेषदुःखनिवृत्ती भवतो न तु कर्मणेति । उच्यते ।

Why, then, do the attainment of the unsurpassed happiness one desires and the complete removal of suffering follow solely from knowledge of the Self as it truly is, and not from action? The answer is:

Text 110

1 · 110

कर्माज्ञानसमुत्थत्वान्नालं मोहापनुत्तये ।

Action arises from ignorance and is not able to remove delusion;

Verse 1.35

1.35

सम्यग्ज्ञानं विरोध्यस्य तामिस्रस्यांशुमानिव ॥ ३५॥

right knowledge, its opposite, is like sunlight to darkness.

Text 112

1 · 112

नन्वात्मज्ञानमप्यविद्योपादानम् । न हि शास्त्रशिष्याचार्याद्यनुपादायात्मज्ञानमात्मानं लभतः इति । नैष दोषः । यत आत्मज्ञानं हि स्वतस्सिद्धपरमार्थात्मवस्तुस्वरूपमात्राशयादेवाविद्यातदुत्पन्नकारकग्रामप्रध्वंसि स्वात्मोपतावेव शास्त्रादपेक्ष्यते नोत्पन्नमविद्यानिवृत्तौ । कर्म पुनः स्वात्मोत्पत्तावुत्पन्नं च । न हिक्रिया कारकनिस्स्पृहा कल्पकोटिव्यवहितफलदानाय स्वात्मानं बिभर्ति साध्यमानमात्ररूपत्वात्तस्याः । न च क्रियात्मज्ञानवत्स्वात्मप्रतिलम्भकाल एव स्वर्गादिफलेन कर्तारं संबध्नाति । आत्मज्ञानं पुनः पुरुषार्थसिद्धौ नोत्पद्यमानस्वरूपव्यतिरेकेणान्यद्रूपान्तरं साधनान्तरं वापेक्षते । कुत एतत् । यतः ।

One may object: “Knowledge of the Self also has ignorance as its basis. No one gains knowledge of the Self without relying on scripture, a student, a teacher, and the like.” This is no defect. Knowledge of the Self destroys ignorance and the entire apparatus of agency that arises from it, because its aim is only the nature of the Self, the supreme reality that is already self-established. It depends on scripture only for the recognition of one’s own Self, not for the production of the Self or the removal of ignorance as a produced effect. Action, however, is undertaken to produce something. An action cannot carry itself forward to yield a result separated from it by countless ages, independently of its instruments and agent, because its very nature is only that of something being accomplished. Nor, unlike knowledge of the Self, does an action connect its agent with a result such as heaven at the very moment the Self is realized. Knowledge of the Self, by contrast, needs no other form or additional means beyond its own arising for the human end to be accomplished. Why is this so? Because:

Text 113

1 · 113

बलवद्धि प्रमाणोत्थं सम्यग्ज्ञानं न बाध्यते ।

Right knowledge arising from a powerful means of knowledge is not overturned;

Verse 1.36

1.36

आकाङ्क्षते न चाप्यान्यद्बाधनं प्रति साधनम् ॥ ३६॥

nor does it depend on any further means to prevent its being overturned.

Text 115

1 · 115

स्वपक्षस्य हेत्ववष्टम्भेन समर्थितत्वानिराशङ्कमुपसंह्रियते ।

Having established his own position by supporting it with reasons, he concludes without hesitation:

Text 116

1 · 116

तस्माद्दुःखोदधेर्हेतोरज्ञानस्यापनुत्तये ।

Therefore, to remove ignorance, the cause of the ocean of suffering,

Verse 1.37

1.37

सम्यग्ज्ञानं सुपर्याप्तं क्रिया चेन्नोक्तहेतुतः ॥ ३७॥

right knowledge alone is fully sufficient; action is not, for the reasons already stated.

Text 118

1 · 118

ननु बलवदपि सम्यग्ज्ञानं सदप्रमाणोत्थेन बाध्यमानमुपलभामहे यत उत्पन्नपरमार्थबोधस्यापि कर्तृत्वभोक्तृत्वरागद्वेषाद्यनवबोधोत्थप्रत्यया आविर्भवन्ति । न ह्यबाधिते सम्यग्ज्ञाने तद्विरुद्धानां प्रत्ययानां सम्भवोऽस्ति । नैतदेवम् । कुतः ।

One may object: “We observe that even powerful right knowledge is overturned by a cognition that lacks authority, since ideas of agency, enjoyment, attachment, aversion, and the like—arising from ignorance—appear even in one who has gained true understanding. Such contrary cognitions cannot occur while right knowledge remains unopposed.” That is not so. Why?

Text 119

1 · 119

बाधितत्वादविद्याया विद्यां सा नैव बाधते ।

Ignorance has been sublated by knowledge; it cannot then sublate knowledge.

Verse 1.38

1.38

तद्वासना निमित्तत्वं यान्ति विद्यास्मृतेर्ध्रुवन् ॥ ३८॥

Its residual impression can only occasion the memory of knowledge, which is certain to prevail.

Text 121

1 · 121

"कर्माज्ञानसमुत्थत्वात् " इत्युक्तो हेतुस्तस्य च समर्थनं पूर्वमेवाभिहितं "हितं सम्प्रेप्सताम् " इत्यादिना । तदभ्युच्चयार्थमविद्यान्वयेन च संसारान्वयित्वं प्रदर्शयिष्यामीत्यत आह ।

The reason already stated—“because action arises from ignorance”—was supported earlier by the passage beginning, “For those who seek to secure what is good.” To reinforce it, he now shows that saṃsāra continues as long as ignorance does:

Text 122

1 · 122

ब्राह्मण्याद्यात्मके देहे लात्वा नात्मेति भावनाम् ।

Taking the body, whose features include being a Brahmin and the like, to be the Self,

Verse 1.39

1.39

श्रुतेः किङ्करतामेति वाङ्मनःकायकर्मसु ॥ ३९॥

one becomes subject to scripture’s commands concerning actions of speech, mind, and body.

Text 124

1 · 124

यस्मात्कर्माज्ञानसमुत्थमेव तस्मात्तद्व्यावृत्तौ निवर्तत इत्युच्यते ।

Since action arises from ignorance, it ceases when ignorance is removed, as is now explained:

Text 125

1 · 125

दग्धाखिलाधिकारश्चेद्ब्रह्मज्ञानाग्निना मुनिः ।

If the sage’s entire field of duty has been burned by the fire of knowledge of Brahman,

Verse 1.40

1.40

वर्तमानः श्रुतेर्मूर्ध्नि नैव स्याद्वेदकिङ्करः ॥ ४०॥

then, standing at the summit of revelation, he is no longer a servant of the Veda.

Text 127

1 · 127

अथेतरो घनतराविद्या पटलसंवीतान्तःकरणोऽङ्गीकृतकर्तृत्वाद्यशेषकर्माधिकारकारणो विधिप्रतिषेधचोदनासंदंशोपदष्टः कर्मसु प्रवर्तमानः ।

But another person, whose mind is veiled by a dense mass of ignorance and who accepts agency and the rest—the grounds for every kind of ritual duty—acts under the pressure of scriptural injunctions and prohibitions.

Text 128

1 · 128

शुभैः प्राप्नोति देवत्वं निषिद्धैर्नारकीं गतिम् ।

Through good actions he attains divine status; through forbidden actions he goes to hell;

Verse 1.41

1.41

उभाभ्यां पुण्यपापाभ्यां मानुष्यं लभतेऽवशः ॥ ४१॥

powerless, he gains human birth through both merit and demerit.

Text 130

1 · 130

आब्रह्मस्तम्बपर्यन्ते घोरे दुःखोदधौ घटीयन्त्रवदारोहावरोहन्यायेनाधममध्यमोत्तमसुखदुःखमोहविद्युच्चपलसंपातदायिनीर्विचित्रयोनीश्चण्डोत्पिञ्जलकश्वसनवेगाभिहताम्भोधिमध्यवर्तिशुष्कालाबुवच्छुभाशुभव्यामिश्रकर्मवायुसमीरितः ।

Propelled by winds of mixed good and bad action, he is tossed through the dreadful ocean of suffering, from Brahmā down to a clump of grass, rising and falling like a water-wheel. Its many births bring sudden flashes of inferior, middling, and superior pleasure, pain, and delusion; he is like a dry gourd beaten by fierce winds in the middle of a roaring sea.

Text 131

1 · 131

एवं चङ्क्रम्यमाणोऽयमविद्याकामकर्मभिः ।

Thus he wanders, driven by ignorance, desire, and action;

Verse 1.42

1.42

पाशितो जायते कामी म्रियते चासुखावृतः ॥ ४२॥

bound by them, he is born full of desire and dies enveloped in misery.

Text 133

1 · 133

यथोक्तेऽर्थ आदरविधानाय प्रमाणोपन्यासः ।

An authoritative text is now cited to affirm what has been said.

Text 134

1 · 134

श्रुतिश्चेमं जगादार्थं कामस्य विनिवृत्तये ।

Revelation also teaches this point, so that desire may cease:

Verse 1.43

1.43

तन्मूला संसृतिर्यस्मात्तन्नाशोऽज्ञानहानतः ॥ ४३॥

“For saṃsāra has desire as its root; it ends when ignorance is destroyed.”

Text 136

1 · 136

का त्वसौ श्रुतिरिति चेत् ।

If one asks, “Which passage of revelation?”

Text 137

1 · 137

"यदा सर्वे प्रमुच्यन्त " "इति नु " इति च वाजिनः ।

the Vājasaneyins say, “When all desires are released,” and “so indeed”;

Verse 1.44

1.44

कामबन्धनमेवेदं व्यासोऽप्याह पदेपदे ॥ ४४॥

Vyāsa too says at every turn that this is bondage to desire.

Text 139

1 · 139

एष संसारपन्था व्याख्यातः । अथेदानीं तद्व्यावृत्तये कर्माण्यारादुपकारकत्वेन यथा मोक्षहेतुतां प्रतिपद्यन्ते तथाभिधीयते ।

The course of saṃsāra has now been explained. Next it is shown how actions, by serving as indirect aids, come to be regarded as a cause of liberation.

Text 140

1 · 140

तस्यैवं दुःखतप्तस्य कथंचित्पुण्यशीलनात् ।

For one scorched by suffering in this way, through some practice of meritorious conduct,

Verse 1.45

1.45

नित्येहाक्षालितधियो वैराग्यं जायते हृदि ॥ ४५॥

dispassion arises in the heart, and discernment becomes steady.

Text 142

1 · 142

कीदृग्वैराग्यमुत्पद्यत इति । उच्यते ।

What kind of dispassion arises? The answer is:

Text 143

1 · 143

नरकाद्भीर्यथास्याभूत्तथा काम्यफलादपि ।

Just as he fears hell, so too he fears the fruits of desire-driven action.

Verse 1.46

1.46

यथार्थदर्शनात्तस्मान्नित्यं कर्म चिकीर्षति ॥ ४६॥

Seeing things as they are, he therefore wishes always to perform obligatory action.

Text 145

1 · 145

एवं नित्यनैमित्तिककर्मानुष्ठानेन ।

By performing obligatory and occasional actions,

Text 146

1 · 146

शुध्यमानं तु तच्चित्तमीश्वरार्पितकर्मभिः ।

his mind is purified through works offered to the Lord;

Verse 1.47

1.47

वैराग्यं ब्रह्मलोकादौ व्यनक्त्यथ सुनिर्मलम् ॥ ४७॥

then it reveals perfectly pure dispassion even toward the world of Brahmā.

Text 148

1 · 148

यस्माद्रजस्तमोमलोपसंसृष्टमेव चित्तं कामबडिशेनाकृष्य विषयदुरन्तसूनास्थानेष निक्षिप्यते तस्मान्नित्यनैमित्तिककर्मानुष्ठानपरिमार्जनेनापविद्धरजस्तमोमलं प्रसन्नमनाकृलं संमार्जितस्फटिकशिलाकल्पं बाह्यविषयहेतुकेन च रागद्वेषात्मकेनातिग्रहबडिशेनानाकृष्यमाणं विधूताशेषकल्मषं प्रत्यङ्मात्रप्रवणं चित्तदर्पणमवतिष्टते । अत इदमभिधीयते ।

When the mind is stained by rajas and tamas, the bait of desire draws it into the perilous snares of sense-objects. But when obligatory and occasional duties have cleansed away those stains, the mind remains clear and undisturbed, like a polished crystal. No longer drawn outward by the strong hooks of attachment and aversion to sense-objects, it is freed of every fault and turned wholly inward. Therefore it is said:

Text 149

1 · 149

व्युत्थिताशेषकामेभ्यो यदा धीरवतिष्ठते ।

When the wise one stands apart from all the desires that had arisen,

Verse 1.48

1.48

तदैव प्रत्यगात्मानं स्वयमेवाविविक्षति ॥ ४८॥

then, by himself, he seeks to enter the inward Self.

Text 151

1 · 151

अतःपरमवसिताधिकाराणि कर्माणि प्रत्यक्प्रवणवत्वसूनौ कृतसंप्रत्तिकानि चरितार्थानि सन्ति ।

From this point the actions whose scope has ended have served their purpose: they have brought the mind to turn inward.

Text 152

1 · 152

प्रत्यक्प्रवणतां बुद्धेः कर्माण्युत्पाद्य शुद्धितः ।

Having purified the mind and made the intellect turn inward, actions

Verse 1.49

1.49

कृतार्थान्यस्तमायान्ति प्रावृडन्ते घना इव ॥ ४९॥

having fulfilled their purpose, pass away like clouds at the end of the rainy season.

Text 154

1 · 154

यतो नित्यकर्मानुष्ठानस्यैष महिमा ।

This, then, is the greatness of performing obligatory duties.

Text 155

1 · 155

तस्मान्मुमुक्षुभिः कार्यमात्मज्ञानाभिलाषिभिः ।

Therefore those who seek liberation and desire knowledge of the Self

Verse 1.50

1.50

नित्यं नैमित्तिकं कर्म सदैवात्मविशुद्धये ॥ ५०॥

should always perform obligatory and occasional action to purify the mind.

Text 157

1 · 157

यथोक्तेऽर्थे सर्वज्ञवचनं प्रमाणम् ।

The teaching of the all-knowing one is authority for this point:

Text 158

1 · 158

"आरुरुक्षोर्मुनेर्योगं कर्म कारणमुच्यते ।

“For the sage who seeks to ascend to yoga, action is said to be the means;

Verse 1.51

1.51

योगारूढस्य तस्यैव शम" एवेति च स्मृतिः ॥ ५१॥

for that same one who has attained yoga, stillness alone is the means,” says the tradition.

Text 160

1 · 160

नित्यकर्मानुष्ठानाद्धर्मोत्पत्तिर्धर्मोत्पत्तेः पापहानिस्ततश्चित्तशुद्धिस्ततः संसारयाथात्म्यावबोधस्ततो वैराग्यं ततो मुमुक्षुत्वं ततस्तदुपायपर्येषणं ततः सर्वकर्मतत्साधनसंन्यासस्ततो योगाभ्यासस्ततश्चित्तस्य प्रत्यक्प्रवणता ततस्तत्त्वमस्यादिवाक्यार्थपरिज्ञानं ततोऽविद्योच्छेदस्ततश्च स्वात्मन्ये वावस्थानं "ब्रह्मैव सन् ब्रह्माप्येति " "विमुक्तश्च विमुच्यते " इति ।

From the performance of obligatory action comes merit; from merit, the removal of sin; then purification of mind; then understanding the true nature of saṃsāra; then dispassion; then the desire for liberation; then the search for its means; then renunciation of all actions and their instruments; then practice of yoga; then the mind’s inward direction; then understanding the meaning of sayings such as “That thou art”; then the destruction of ignorance; and then abiding in one’s own Self alone: “Being Brahman, one reaches Brahman”; “one who has been freed is fully liberated.”

Text 161

1 · 161

पारम्पर्येण कर्मैवं स्यादविद्यानिवृत्तये ।

It may be said that action can remove ignorance by a gradual process;

Verse 1.52

1.52

ज्ञानवन्नाविरोधित्वात्कर्माविद्यां निरस्यति ॥ ५२॥

unlike knowledge, action is not opposed to ignorance and can therefore dispel it.

Text 163

1 · 163

न च कर्मणः कार्यमण्वपि मुक्तौ संभाव्यते नापि मुक्तौ यत्संभवति तत्कर्मापेक्षते । तदुच्यते ।

No action, even the least, can be involved in liberation, nor does liberation depend on any action. This is explained:

Text 164

1 · 164

उत्पाद्यमाप्यं संस्कार्यं विकार्यं च क्रियाफलम् ।

The fruits of action are production, attainment, purification, or transformation;

Verse 1.53

1.53

नैवं मुक्तिर्यतस्तस्मात्कर्म तस्या न साधनम् ॥ ५३॥

liberation is none of these; action is therefore not its means.

Text 166

1 · 166

एवं तावत्केवलं कर्म साक्षादविद्यापनुत्तये न पर्याप्तमिति प्रपञ्चितम् । मुक्तौ च मुमुक्षुज्ञानतद्विषयस्वाभाव्यानुरोधेन सर्वप्रकारस्यापि कर्मणोऽसंभव उक्तो "हितं संप्रेप्सताम् " इत्यादिना ।

It has thus been explained that action alone is not enough to remove ignorance directly. And, as shown by the passage beginning “For those who seek to secure what is good,” no action of any kind can be combined with liberation, which accords with the nature of the knowledge sought by the seeker.

Text 167

1 · 167

यादृशश्चारादुपकारकत्वेन ज्ञानोत्पत्तौ कर्मणां समुच्चयः संभवति तथा प्रतिपादितम् । अविद्योच्छित्तौ तु लब्धात्मस्वभावस्यात्मज्ञानस्यैवासाधारणं साधकतमत्वं नान्यस्य प्रधानभूतस्य गुणभूतस्य चेत्येतदधुनोच्यते ।

It has also been shown how actions may be combined with knowledge as indirect aids to its arising. But when ignorance is to be destroyed, knowledge of the Self—whose nature is already attained—is uniquely the most effective means, and nothing else, whether principal or auxiliary. This is now stated.

Text 168

1 · 168

तत्र ज्ञानं गुणभूतं तावदहेतुरित्येतदाह ।

First he says that knowledge, as a mere auxiliary, is not the cause:

Text 169

1 · 169

संनिपत्य न च ज्ञानं कर्माज्ञानं निरस्यति ।

Knowledge and action do not come together to remove ignorance;

Verse 1.54

1.54

साध्यसाधनभावत्वादेककालानवस्थितेः ॥ ५४॥

they cannot remain at the same time, since one is a means and the other its result.

Text 171

1 · 171

समप्रधानयोरप्यसंभव एव ।

Nor can they exist together as equally principal means.

Text 172

1 · 172

बाध्यबाधकभावाच्च पञ्चास्योरणयोरिव ।

For they oppose and sublate one another, like a lion and an elephant in combat;

Verse 1.55

1.55

एकदेशानवस्थानान्न समुच्चयता तयोः ॥ ५५॥

the two cannot be combined, since they cannot abide in the same place.

Text 174

1 · 174

कुतो बाध्यबाधकभावः । यस्मात् ।

Why do they oppose one another? Because

Text 175

1 · 175

अयथावस्त्वविद्या स्याद्विद्या तस्या विरोधिनी ।

ignorance is a false apprehension of things; knowledge contradicts it.

Verse 1.56

1.56

समुच्चयस्तयोरेवं रविशार्वरयोरिव ॥ ५६॥

Their conjunction is therefore impossible, like day and night.

Text 177

1 · 177

तस्मादकारकब्रह्मात्मनि परिसमाप्तावबोधस्याशेषकर्म चोदनानामचोद्यस्वाभाव्यात्कुण्ठता । कथं तत् ।

Once knowledge is complete in the non-agent Brahman-Self, all injunctions to action lose their force, since action is foreign to the Self’s nature.

Text 178

1 · 178

अभिधीयते ।

How so? It is explained:

Text 179

1 · 179

बृहस्पतिसवे यद्वत्क्षत्रियो न प्रवर्तते ।

Just as a Kṣatriya does not undertake the Bṛhaspati sacrifice,

Verse 1.57

1.57

ब्राह्मणत्वाद्यहंमानी विप्री वा क्षत्रकर्मणि ॥ ५७॥

so a Brahmin who thinks “I am a Brahmin” does not undertake the duties of a Kṣatriya.

Text 181

1 · 181

यथायं दृष्टान्त एवं दार्ष्टान्तिकोऽपीत्येतदाह ।

He says that the illustration corresponds to the point being illustrated:

Text 182

1 · 182

विदेहो वीतसन्देहो नेतिनेत्यवशेषितः ।

The one who is free of the body, free of doubt, and left with the teaching “not this, not this,”

Verse 1.58

1.58

देहाद्यनात्मदृक्तद्वत्क्रियां वीक्षतेऽपि न ॥ ५८॥

sees the body and the rest as not-Self and, like that person, does not regard action.

Text 184

1 · 184

तस्यार्थस्याविष्करणार्थमुदाहरणम् ।

An example is given to make this point clear:

Text 185

1 · 185

मृत्स्नेभके यथेभत्वं शिशुरध्यस्य वल्गति ।

A child imagines a clay elephant to be a real elephant and jumps about;

Verse 1.59

1.59

अध्यस्यात्मानि देहादीन्मूढस्तद्वत्विचेष्टते ॥ ५९॥

likewise, the ignorant person acts after superimposing the body and the rest upon the Self.

Text 187

1 · 187

न च वयं ज्ञानकर्मणोः सर्वत्रैव प्रत्याचक्ष्महे । यत्र प्रयोज्यप्रयोजकभावो ज्ञानकर्मणोस्तत्र नास्मत्पित्रापि शक्यते निवारयितुम् । तत्र विभागप्रदर्शनायोदाहरणं प्रदर्श्यते ।

We do not reject every possible connection between knowledge and action. Where knowledge serves as an instrument that enables an action, no one—not even our father—could prevent that connection. To show how the two are divided, an example is given:

Text 188

1 · 188

स्थाणुं चोरधियालाय भीतो यद्वत्पलायते ।

A frightened person who mistakes a post for a thief runs away;

Verse 1.60

1.60

बुद्ध्यादिभिस्तथात्मानं भ्रान्तोऽध्यारीप्य चेष्टते ॥ ६०॥

so the confused person acts after mistakenly superimposing intellect and the rest upon the Self.

Text 190

1 · 190

एवं यत्रयत्र ज्ञानकर्मणोः प्रयोज्यप्रयोजकभावस्तत्र सर्वत्रायं न्यायः । यत्र तु न समकालं नापि क्रमेणोपपद्यते समुच्चयः सविषय उच्यते ।

This rule applies wherever knowledge and action stand in a relation of means and end. The case in which they cannot be combined, either at once or in sequence, is now explained.

Text 191

1 · 191

स्थाणोः सतत्त्वविज्ञानं यथा नाङ्गं पलायते ।

Knowing the true nature of a post is not an auxiliary to running away;

Verse 1.61

1.61

आत्मनस्तत्त्वविज्ञानं तद्वन्नाङ्गं क्रियाविधौ ॥ ६१॥

likewise, knowledge of the Self’s true nature is not an auxiliary to an injunction to act.

Text 193

1 · 193

यस्माद्गुणस्यैतत्स्वाभाव्यम् ।

This follows from the nature of an auxiliary:

Text 194

1 · 194

यद्धि यस्यानुरोधेन स्वभावमनुवर्तते ।

whatever follows another thing in accordance with its nature

Verse 1.62

1.62

तत्तस्य गुणभूतं स्यान्न प्रधानाद्गुणो यतः ॥ ६२॥

is subordinate to it; an auxiliary cannot be the principal.

Text 196

1 · 196

यस्मात् ।

For this reason:

Text 197

1 · 197

कर्मप्रकरणाकाङ्क्षिज्ञानं कर्मगुणो भवेत् ।

knowledge that depends on a ritual context becomes an accessory of ritual action;

Verse 1.63

1.63

यद्धि प्रकरणे यस्य तत्तदङ्गं प्रचक्षते ॥ ६३॥

what belongs to a ritual context is called one of its limbs.

Text 199

1 · 199

स्वरूपलाभमात्रेण यत्त्वविद्यां निहन्ति नः ।

But knowledge, which destroys our ignorance merely by its own arising,

Verse 1.64

1.64

न तदङ्गं प्रधानं वा ज्ञानं स्यात्कर्मणः क्वचित् ॥ ६४॥

can never be either an auxiliary or a principal part of action.

Text 201

1 · 201

समुच्चयपक्षवादिनाप्यवश्यमेतदभ्युपगन्तव्यम् ।

Even the proponent of combining knowledge and action must necessarily accept this;

Text 202

1 · 202

यस्मात् ।

for:

Text 203

1 · 203

अज्ञानमनिराकुर्वज्ज्ञानमेव न सिध्यति ।

knowledge cannot be established while ignorance remains unremoved;

Verse 1.65

1.65

विपन्नकारकग्रामं ज्ञानं कर्म न ढौकते ॥ ६५॥

action, with its whole apparatus of agent and instruments, does not reach knowledge.

Text 205

1 · 205

इदं चापरं कारणं ज्ञानकर्मणोः समुच्चयनिबर्हि ।

Here is another reason why knowledge and action cannot be combined:

Text 206

1 · 206

हेतुस्वरूपकार्याणि प्रकाशतमसोरिव ।

Their causes, natures, and effects are opposed, like light and darkness;

Verse 1.66

1.66

विरोधीनि ततो नास्ति साङ्गत्यं ज्ञानकर्मणोः ॥ ६६॥

therefore knowledge and action cannot go together.

Text 208

1 · 208

एवमुपसंहृते केचित्स्वसम्प्रदायबलावष्टम्भादाहुर्यदे तद्वेदान्तवाक्यादहं ब्रह्मेति विज्ञानं समुत्पद्यते तन्नैव स्वोत्पत्तिमात्रेणाज्ञानं निरस्यति । किं तर्हि । अहन्यहनि द्राघीयसा कालेनोपासीनस्य सतो भावनोपचयान्निश्शेषम् अज्ञानमपगच्छति "देवो भूत्वा देवानप्येति " इति श्रुतेः । अपरे तु ब्रुवते वेदान्तवाक्यजनितमहं ब्रह्मेति विज्ञानं संसर्गात्मकत्वादात्मवस्तुयाथात्म्यावगाह्येव न भवति । किं तर्हि ।

Having concluded thus, some, relying on the strength of their own tradition, say: “The knowledge ‘I am Brahman’ arises from a Vedānta sentence, but its mere arising does not remove ignorance. What does? After one has meditated for a long time, day after day, the increase of contemplation removes ignorance completely, as scripture says, ‘Having become a god, one reaches the gods.’ Others say that the knowledge ‘I am Brahman’ produced by a Vedānta sentence does not penetrate the true nature of the Self, because it is relational in form. What does?”

Text 209

1 · 209

एतदेव गङ्गास्रोतोवत्सततमभ्यस्यतोऽन्यदेवावाक्यार्थात्मकं विज्ञानान्तरमुत्पद्यते । अस्य पक्षद्वयस्य निवृत्तय इदमभिधीयते ।

“By constantly repeating that very [sentence], like the flow of the Ganges, a different knowledge arises, whose content is no longer merely the sentence.” This is stated to refute both views.

Text 210

1 · 210

सकृत्प्रवृत्त्या मृद्नाति क्रियाकारकरूपभृत् ।

Knowledge arising from scripture crushes ignorance at a single stroke, though it appears in the form of an act and its agent;

Verse 1.67

1.67

अज्ञानमागमज्ञानं साङ्गत्यं नास्त्यतोऽनयोः ॥ ६७॥

therefore scriptural knowledge and action cannot be joined.

Text 212

1 · 212

एवं तावदनानात्वे ब्रह्मणि ज्ञानकर्मणोः समुच्चयो निराकृतः । अथाधुना पक्षान्तराभ्युपगमेनापि प्रत्यवस्थाने पूर्ववदनाश्वासो यथा तथाभिधीयते ।

Thus the combination of knowledge and action has been rejected for Brahman, which is free from multiplicity. Now, even if another position is accepted, it is shown that it remains just as uncertain as before.

Text 213

1 · 213

अनुत्सारितनानात्वं ब्रह्म यस्यापि वादिनः ।

Even for a proponent whose Brahman has not been freed from multiplicity,

Verse 1.68

1.68

तन्मतेनापि दुस्साध्यो ज्ञानकर्मसमुच्चयः ॥ ६८॥

the combination of knowledge and action is difficult to establish on his own view.

Text 215

1 · 215

तस्य विभागोक्तिर्दूषणविभागप्रज्ञप्तये ।

The alternatives are stated to lay out the separate objections:

Text 216

1 · 216

ब्रह्मात्मा वा भवेत्तस्य यदि वानात्मरूपकम् ।

For him, Brahman may be the Self, or it may have the nature of not-Self;

Verse 1.69

1.69

आत्मानाप्तिर्भवेन्मोहादितरस्याप्यनात्मनः ॥ ६९॥

in the latter case, because of delusion, the Self would not be attained, nor would the other, not-Self, be the Self.

Text 218

1 · 218

तत्र यदि तावद्वास्तवेनैव वृत्तेन ब्रह्म प्राप्तमात्मस्वाभाव्यात्केवलमासुरमोहापिधानमात्रमेवानाप्तिनिमित्तं तस्मिन्पक्षे ।

If Brahman is already truly attained because it is the nature of the Self, and its non-attainment is caused only by a demonic delusion that veils it, then, on that view,

Text 219

1 · 219

मोहापिधानभङ्गाय नैव कर्माणि कारणम् ।

action is not the means to break the veil of delusion;

Verse 1.70

1.70

ज्ञानेनैव फलावाप्तेस्तत्र कर्म निरर्थकम् ॥ ७०॥

knowledge alone brings the result, and action there is pointless.

Text 221

1 · 221

अनात्मरूपके तु ब्रह्मणि न कर्म साधनभावं प्रतिपद्यते नापि ज्ञानं कर्मसमुच्चितमसमुच्चितं वा यस्मादन्यस्य स्वत एव साधकस्य ब्रह्मणोऽप्यन्यत्वं स्वत एव सिद्धम् ।

But if Brahman has the nature of not-Self, action cannot become its means, nor can knowledge—combined with action or without it—be its means, since Brahman is already established as different from the one who independently acts to attain it.

Text 222

1 · 222

तत्रैवम् ।

In that case,

Text 223

1 · 223

अन्यस्यान्यात्मताप्राप्तौ न क्वचिद्धेतुसंभवः ।

there can be no cause by which one thing becomes the Self of another;

Verse 1.71

1.71

तस्मिन् सत्यपि नो नष्टः परात्मानं प्रपद्यते ॥ ७१॥

even when that other thing exists, it is not destroyed, nor does the Self attain another Self.

Text 225

1 · 225

अपरस्मिंस्तु पक्षे विधिः ।

On the other alternative, however, an injunction might be possible:

Text 226

1 · 226

परमात्मानुकूलेन ज्ञानाभ्यासेन दुःखिनः ।

Even dualists who suffer could be freed through the practice of knowledge that accords with the Supreme Self,

Verse 1.72

1.72

द्वैतिनोऽपि प्रमुच्येरन्न परात्मविरोधिना ॥ ७२॥

but not through action that opposes the Supreme Self.

Text 228

1 · 228

इतरस्मिंस्तु पक्षे विधेरेवानवकाशत्वम् । कथम् ।

On the first alternative, however, there is no room for an injunction at all. How?

Text 229

1 · 229

समस्तव्यस्तभूतस्य ब्रह्मण्येवावतिष्ठतः ।

For one who abides in Brahman, seeing all beings as both distinct and not distinct,

Verse 1.73

1.73

ब्रूत कर्मणि को हेतुः सर्वानन्यत्वदर्शिनः ॥ ७३॥

what could prompt action for one who sees that nothing is other than the Self?

Text 231

1 · 231

सर्वकर्मनिमित्तसंभवासंभवाभ्यां सर्वकर्मसङ्करश्च प्राप्नोति । यस्मात् ।

If causes for every action are present or absent in the same way, all actions become confused together. For:

Text 232

1 · 232

सर्वजात्यादिमत्त्वेऽस्य नितरां हेतसंभवः ।

If this [Self] possessed every caste and other attribute, there would be abundant cause for action;

Verse 1.74

1.74

विशेषं ह्यनुपादाय कर्म नैव प्रवर्तते ॥ ७४॥

for action never proceeds without taking a distinction as its basis.

Text 234

1 · 234

स्याद्विधिरध्यात्मभिमानादिति चेन्नैवम् । यस्मात् ।

If one says that injunction rests on identification with the inner Self, that is not so, because:

Text 235

1 · 235

न चाध्यात्माभिमानोऽपि विदुषोऽस्त्यासुरत्वतः ।

the knower has no identification with the body and the rest; such identification is demonic.

Verse 1.75

1.75

विदुषोऽप्यासुरश्चेत्स्यान्निष्फलं ब्रह्मदर्शनम् ॥ ७५॥

If even the knower were demonic in this way, the vision of Brahman would be fruitless.

Text 237

1 · 237

अज्ञानकार्यत्वान्न समकालं नापि क्रमेण ज्ञानकर्मणोर्वस्त्ववस्तु तन्त्रत्वात्सङ्गतिरस्तीत्येवं निराकृतोऽपि काशं कुशं वावलम्ब्याह ॥

Since knowledge and action depend respectively on reality and unreality, they cannot be joined either simultaneously or in sequence; action arises from ignorance. Yet, grasping at a last straw, someone objects:

Text 238

1 · 238

अथाध्यात्मं पुनर्यायादाश्रितो मूढतां भवेत् ।

“If he should turn again toward the inner Self, he would become deluded.

Verse 1.76

1.76

स करोत्येव कर्माणि को ह्यज्ञं विनिवारयेत् ॥ ७६॥

He will go on performing actions; for who can restrain an ignorant person?”

Text 240

1 · 240

सिद्धत्वाच्च न साध्यम् । यतः ।

And because liberation is already established, it is not something to be accomplished. For:

Text 241

1 · 241

सामान्येतररूपाभ्यां कर्मात्मैवास्य योगिनः ।

For this yogin, the Self itself is action, in a general way and in a distinctive way;

Verse 1.77

1.77

निश्श्वासोच्छ्वासवत्तस्मान्न नियोगमपेक्षते । ॥ ७७॥

therefore it needs no injunction, just as breathing in and out needs none.

Text 243

1 · 243

अस्तु तर्हि भिन्नाभिन्नात्मकं ब्रह्म । तथा च सति ज्ञानकर्मणो संभवतो भेदाभेदविषयत्वात्तयोः । तत्र तावदयं पक्ष एव न संभवति । किं कारणम् । न हि भिन्नोऽयमित्यभेदबुद्धिमनिराकृत्य भेदबुद्धिः पदार्थमालिङ्गते । एवं ह्यनभ्युपगमे भिन्नाभिन्नपदार्थयोरलौकिकत्वं प्रसज्येत । अथ निष्प्रमाणकमप्याश्रीयते तदप्युभयपक्षाभ्युपगमादभेदपक्षे दुःखिब्रह्म स्यादत आह ।

Suppose, then, that Brahman is both different and non-different, so that knowledge and action could occur because they concern difference and non-difference. This view is impossible. Why? Unless the cognition of non-difference is first set aside, the cognition “this is different” cannot grasp its object. If this is not admitted, both difference and non-difference would be impossible to know. Even if one accepts a view unsupported by evidence, Brahman would suffer because non-difference is also part of that view. Thus he says:

Text 244

1 · 244

भिन्नाभिन्नं विशेषैश्चेद्दुःखि स्याद्ब्रह्म ते ध्रुवम् ।

If your Brahman is both different and non-different in its particular aspects, it will surely be subject to suffering;

Verse 1.78

1.78

अशेषदुःखिता च स्यादहो प्रज्ञात्मवादिनाम् ॥ ७८॥

indeed, it would be subject to every kind of suffering—an astonishing doctrine for those who claim the Self is pure consciousness.

Text 246

1 · 246

तस्मात्सम्यगेवाभिहितं न ज्ञानकर्मणोः समुच्चय इत्युपसंह्रियते ।

Therefore the conclusion is correct: knowledge and action are not to be combined.

Text 247

1 · 247

तमोऽङ्गत्वं यथा भानोरग्नेश्शीताङ्गता यथा ।

Just as darkness cannot be an attribute of the sun, nor coldness an attribute of fire,

Verse 1.79

1.79

वारिणश्चोष्णता यद्वज्ज्ञानस्यैवं क्रियाङ्गता ॥ ७९॥

nor heat an attribute of water, so action cannot be an attribute of knowledge.

Text 249

1 · 249

यथोक्तोपपत्तिबलेनैव पूर्वपक्षस्योत्सारितत्वाद्वक्तव्यं नावशेषितमित्यतः प्रतिपत्तिकर्मवत्पूर्वपक्षपरिहाराय यत्किञ्चिद्वक्तव्यमित्यत इदमभिधीयते ।

Since the foregoing reasoning has already removed the opponent’s position, there is nothing further to state. Yet, to answer any remaining objection as one answers an argument with its own counter-argument, he adds:

Text 250

1 · 250

"मुक्तेः क्रियाभिः सिद्धत्वात् " इत्याद्यनुचितं बहु ।

Much of what you said—beginning with “liberation is accomplished by actions”—

Verse 1.80

1.80

यदभाणि तदन्याय्यं यथा तदधुनोच्यते ॥ ८०॥

was unreasonable. I shall now explain why.

Text 252

1 · 252

योऽयं काम्यानां प्रतिषिद्धानां च त्यागः प्रतिज्ञायते सा प्रतिज्ञा तावन्न शक्यतेऽनुष्ठातुम् । किं कारणम् ।

First, the promise to abandon desire-driven and prohibited actions cannot be carried out. Why?

Text 253

1 · 253

कर्मणो हि निर्वृत्तात्मनो द्वाभ्यां प्रकाराभ्यां निवृत्तिः संभवत्यारब्धफलस्योपभोगेनानारब्धफलस्याशुभस्य प्रायश्चित्तैरिति । तृतीयोऽपि त्यागप्रकारोऽकर्त्रात्मावबोधात् स त्वात्मज्ञानानभ्युपगमाद्भवता नाभ्युपगम्यते । तत्र यान्यनुपभुक्तफलान्यनारब्धफलानि तानीश्वरेणापि केनचिदपि न शक्यन्ते परित्यक्तुम् ।

Completed actions can cease in two ways: the fruit of an action whose result has begun is exhausted by experience, while harmful action whose result has not begun is removed by expiation. There is also a third way of relinquishment—knowledge of the Self as non-agent—but you do not accept it, since you reject knowledge of the Self. Of these, no one—not even the Lord—can abandon actions whose results have not begun and have not been experienced.

Text 254

1 · 254

अथारब्धफलानि त्यज्यन्ते तान्यपि न शक्यन्ते त्यक्तुम् ।

If one tries to abandon actions whose results have begun, those too cannot be abandoned.

Text 255

1 · 255

किं कारणम् । अनिवृत्तेः । अनिर्वृत्तं हि चिकीर्षितं कर्म शक्यते त्यक्तुं प्रवृत्तिनिवृत्ती प्रति कर्तुः स्वातन्त्र्यात् । निर्वृत्ते तु कर्मणि तदसंभवाद्दुरनुष्ठेयः प्रतिज्ञानार्थः । अशक्यप्रतिज्ञानाच्च । न च शक्यते प्रतिज्ञातुं यावज्जीवं काम्यानि प्रतिषिद्धानि च कर्माणि न करिष्यामीति सुनिपुणानामपि सूक्ष्मापराधदर्शनात् । प्रमाणाभावाच्च ।

Why? Because they have already been performed. One can abandon an action not yet performed, since an agent is free to undertake it or refrain; once the action is complete, that choice is no longer possible. Thus the promise is hard to keep. Nor can one promise not to perform any desire-driven or prohibited action for the rest of one’s life: even the most careful people discover subtle faults in themselves. There is no authority for such a promise either.

Text 256

1 · 256

न च प्रमाणमस्ति मोक्षकामो नित्यनैमित्तिके कर्मणी कुर्यात्काम्यप्रतिषिद्धे च वर्जयेदारब्धफले चोपभोगेन क्षपयेदिति । आनन्त्याच्च । न चोपचितानां कर्मणामियत्तास्ति संसारस्यानादित्वात् । न च काम्यैः प्रतिषिधैर्वा तेषां निवृत्तिरस्ति शुद्ध्यशुद्धिसाम्ये सत्यविरोधादित्याह ।

Nor is there any authority for saying that one seeking liberation should perform obligatory and occasional rites, avoid desire-driven and prohibited actions, and exhaust the fruits of actions already begun by experiencing them. Moreover, no limit can be assigned to the actions accumulated, since saṃsāra has no beginning. Desire-driven and prohibited actions do not cancel one another, because they are not opposed to one another even when their purity and impurity differ. This is stated:

Text 257

1 · 257

न कृत्स्नकाम्यसंत्यागोऽनन्तत्वात्कर्तुमिष्यते ।

Complete renunciation of all desire-driven action is not considered possible, because it is infinite;

Verse 1.81

1.81

निषिद्धकर्मणश्चेत्तु व्यतीतानन्तजन्मसु ॥ ८१॥

and the same is true of prohibited action accumulated over infinitely many past births.

Text 259

1 · 259

स्यान्मतं व्यतीताननन्तजन्मोपात्तानां कर्मणाम् ।

One might hold that actions accumulated through infinitely many past births

Text 260

1 · 260

क्षयो नित्येन तेषां चेत्प्रायश्चित्तैर्यथैनसः ।

are exhausted by obligatory rites, just as sins are removed by expiations;

Verse 1.82

1.82

निष्फलत्वान्न नित्येन काम्यादेर्विनिवारणाम् ॥ ८२॥

but obligatory action cannot remove desire-driven action, since it has no such effect.

Text 262

1 · 262

प्रमाणाभावाच्च । कथम् ।

There is no authority for that claim either. How?

Text 263

1 · 263

पापापनुत्तये वाक्यात्प्रायश्चित्तं यथा तथा ।

Just as a scriptural statement establishes expiation as a means of removing sin,

Verse 1.83

1.83

गभ्यते काम्यहानार्थं नित्यं कर्म न वाक्यतः ॥ ८३॥

there is no scriptural statement establishing obligatory action as a means to abandon desire-driven action.

Text 265

1 · 265

अथापि स्यात्काम्यैरेव काम्यानां पूर्वजन्मोपचितानां क्षयो भविष्यतीति । तन्न । यतः ।

One might further say that desire-driven actions themselves will exhaust those from previous births. That is wrong, because:

Text 266

1 · 266

पाप्मनां पाप्मभिर्नास्ति यथैवेह निराक्रिया ।

just as sin is not removed by other sins in this world,

Verse 1.84

1.84

काम्यैरपि तथैवास्तु काम्यानामविरोधतः ॥ ८४॥

so desire-driven actions do not remove other desire-driven actions; they do not oppose one another.

Text 268

1 · 268

एवं तावत् "मुक्तेः क्रियाभिः सिद्धत्वात् " इति निराकृतम् ॥

Thus the view that liberation is accomplished through actions has been refuted.

Text 269

1 · 269

अथात्मज्ञानस्य सद्भावे प्रमाणासंभव उक्तस्तत्परिहारायाह ।

He now answers the claim that there is no authority establishing knowledge of the Self.

Text 270

1 · 270

श्रुतयस्स्मृतिभिस्सामानन्त्यात्कामिनामिह ।

Although there are many passages in revelation and tradition concerning those who desire results,

Verse 1.85

1.85

विदधत्युरुयत्नेन कर्मातो बहुकामदम् ॥ ८५॥

they prescribe action with great insistence and promise many rewards.

Text 272

1 · 272

न च बाहुल्यं प्रामाण्ये कारणभावं प्रतिपद्यते । अत आह ।

Multiplicity does not make a statement authoritative. Therefore he says:

Text 273

1 · 273

प्रामाण्याय न बाहुल्यं न ह्येकत्र प्रमाणताम् ।

Multiplicity is not needed for authority; the means of knowledge do not all range over one and the same object;

Verse 1.86

1.86

वस्तुन्यटन्ति मानानि त्वेकत्रैकस्य मानता ॥ ८६॥

each means of knowledge has its validity with respect to its own object.

Text 275

1 · 275

यत्तूक्तं "यत्नतो वीक्षमाणोऽपि " इति -- तत्रापि भवत एवापराधः । कस्मात् । यतः ।

What you said—“even after searching carefully”—is your own fault. Why? Because:

Text 276

1 · 276

"परीक्ष्य लोकान् " इत्याद्या आत्मज्ञानविधायिनीः ।

Why do you not hear the good passages of revelation, beginning with “Having examined the worlds,” which prescribe knowledge of the Self

Verse 1.87

1.87

नैष्कर्म्यप्रवणास्साध्वीः श्रुतीः किं न शृणोषि ताः ॥८७॥

and direct one toward actionlessness?

Text 278

1 · 278

ननु " आत्मेत्येवोपासीत " "आत्मा वा अरे द्रष्टव्यः" इत्यपूर्वविधिश्रुतेः पुरुषस्यात्मदर्शनक्रियायां नियोगोऽवसीयत इति । नैवम् ।

One may object that statements such as “One should contemplate the Self as the Self” and “the Self should indeed be seen” are novel injunctions, so a person is enjoined to perform the act of seeing the Self. This is not so.

Text 279

1 · 279

अपुरुषतन्त्रत्वाद्वस्तुयाथात्म्यज्ञानस्य सकलानर्थबीजात्मानवबोधोत्सारिणो मुक्तिहेतोरिति ।

True knowledge of reality is not under a person’s control; it removes ignorance, the seed of every misfortune, and is itself the cause of liberation.

Text 280

1 · 280

विध्यभ्युपगमेऽपि नापूर्वविधिरयम् । अत आह ।

Even if an injunction is admitted, this is not a novel injunction. He says:

Text 281

1 · 281

नियमः परिसंख्या वा विध्यर्थोऽपि भवेद्यतः ।

The force of the injunction may be restriction or exclusion;

Verse 1.88

1.88

अनात्मादर्शनेनैव परात्मानमुपास्महे ॥ ८८॥

we contemplate the Supreme Self precisely by not seeing the not-Self.

Text 283

1 · 283

यच्चोक्तं "विश्वासो नान्यतोऽस्ति नः" इति -- तदपि निद्रातुरचेतसा त्वया स्वप्नायमानेन प्रलपितम् । किं कारणम् । न हि वयं प्रमाणबलेनैकात्म्यं प्रतिपद्यामह ऐकात्म्यस्य स्वत एवानुभवमात्रात्मकत्वात् । अत एव सर्वप्रमाणावतारासंभवं वक्ष्यति ।

What you said—“we trust no other source”—was also a dreamlike utterance from a mind drowsy with ignorance. Why? We do not arrive at non-duality through the force of a means of knowledge; oneness is itself nothing but direct experience. For this very reason he will show that no means of knowledge can operate [on what is already self-evident].

Text 284

1 · 284

प्रमाणव्यवस्थायाश्चनुभवमात्राश्रयत्वात् ।

For the ordering of the means of knowledge itself rests on direct experience.

Text 285

1 · 285

अत आह ।

Therefore he says:

Text 286

1 · 286

वाक्यैकगम्यं यद्वस्तु नान्यस्मात्तत्र विश्वसेत् ।

If a thing can be known only through a sentence, one should trust no other source concerning it;

Verse 1.89

1.89

नाऽप्रमेये स्वतस्सिद्धेऽविश्वासः कथमात्मनि ॥ ८९॥

but how could one fail to trust the Self, which is self-established and not an object of knowledge?

Text 288

1 · 288

यदप्युक्तं "अन्तरेण विधिम् " इति --

As for what you said—“without an injunction”—

Text 289

1 · 289

तदप्यबुद्धिपूर्वकमिव नः प्रतिभाति ।

that too seems to us to have been said without due thought.

Text 290

1 · 290

यस्मात्कालान्तरफलदायिषु कर्मस्वेतद्घटते ।

Such reasoning applies to actions whose fruits come at a later time.

Text 291

1 · 291

आत्मलाभकाल एव फलदायिनि त्वात्मज्ञाने नैतत्समञ्जसमित्याह ।

It is not fitting for knowledge of the Self, whose fruit is given at the very moment the Self is attained. He says:

Text 292

1 · 292

ज्ञानात्फले ह्यवाप्तेऽस्मिन्प्रत्यक्षे भवघातिनि ।

When knowledge has attained its immediate result, which destroys saṃsāra,

Verse 1.90

1.90

उपकाराय तन्नेति न न्याय्यं भाति नो वचः ॥ ९०॥

it is unreasonable to say that this knowledge is of no benefit.

Text 294

1 · 294

यदपि जैमिनीयं वचनमुद्घाटयसि -- तदपि तद्विवक्षापरिज्ञानादेवोद्भाव्यते । किं कारणम् । यतो न जैमिनेरयमभिप्राय आम्नायः । सर्व एव क्रियार्थ इति ।

You cite the words of Jaimini, but their intended meaning must first be understood. Jaimini did not mean that the Veda is concerned only with action; for if he had, he would not have composed the profound inquiry into the meaning of all Vedānta, the Śārīraka Sūtras, which reveal the true nature of Brahman alone.

Text 295

1 · 295

यदि ह्ययमभिप्रायोऽभविष्यत् " अथातो ब्रह्मजिज्ञासा ।

“Then, therefore, the inquiry into Brahman”;

Text 296

1 · 296

जन्माद्यस्य यतः " इत्येवमादिब्रह्मवस्तुस्वरूपमात्रयाथात्म्यप्रकाशनपरं गम्भीरन्यायसंदृब्धं सर्ववेदान्तार्थमीमांसनं श्रीमच्छारीरकं नासूत्रयिष्यत् । असूत्रयच्च ।

“that from which the origin and the rest [of the universe proceed]”—and the other sūtras: he did compose them.

Text 297

1 · 297

तस्माज्जैमिनेरेवायमभिप्रायो यथैव विधिवाक्यानां स्वार्थमात्रे प्रामाण्यमेवमैकात्म्यवाक्यानामप्यनधिगत वस्तुपरिच्छेदसाम्यादिति । अत इदमभिधीयते ।

Jaimini’s own view is that, just as injunctions are authoritative regarding their own subject, so too are sentences about non-duality, since they disclose a thing not previously known. Hence it is said:

Text 298

1 · 298

अधिचोदनं य आम्नायस्तस्यैव स्यात्क्रियार्थता ।

The Veda that concerns injunctions may indeed be directed toward action;

Verse 1.91

1.91

तत्त्वमस्यादिवाक्यानां ब्रूत कर्मार्थता कथम् ॥ ९१॥

explain, then, how sentences such as “That thou art” can be directed toward action.

Text 300

1 · 300

अपि च । ऐकात्म्यपक्ष इवादृष्टार्थकर्मसु भवत्पक्षेऽपि प्रवृत्तिर्दुर्लक्ष्या । यतः ।

Moreover, even on your view, action toward unseen results is difficult to explain, just as it is on the view of non-duality. For:

Text 301

1 · 301

स्वर्गं यियासुर्जुहुयादग्निहोत्रं यथाविधि ।

A person who wishes to reach heaven should duly offer the Agnihotra;

Verse 1.92

1.92

देहाद्व्युत्थापितस्यैवं कर्तृत्वं जैमिनेः कथम् ॥ ९२॥

how could Jaimini prompt one who has been separated from the body [as the Self is claimed to be]?

Text 303

1 · 303

न च प्रत्याख्याताशेषशरीरादिकर्मसाधनस्वभावस्यात्ममात्रस्य कर्मस्वधिकारः । यस्मात् ।

The Self alone, whose nature excludes the body and every instrument of action, is not eligible for ritual action. Why?

Text 304

1 · 304

सर्वप्रमाणासंभाव्यो ह्यहंवृत्त्यैकसाधनः ।

It cannot be established by any means of knowledge and is known only through the sense of “I”;

Verse 1.93

1.93

युष्मदर्थमनादित्सुर्जैमिनिः प्रेर्यते कथम् ॥ ९३॥

how could Jaimini prompt it, when it does not take up the object “you”?

Text 306

1 · 306

प्रवृत्तिकारणाभावाच्च । यस्मात् ।

There is no cause that could prompt it to act. For:

Text 307

1 · 307

सुखदुःखादिभिर्योग आत्मनो नाहमेक्ष्यते ।

The Self is not seen to be connected with pleasure, pain, or the like;

Verse 1.94

1.94

पराक्त्वात्प्रत्यगात्मत्वाज्जैमिनिः प्रेर्यते कथम् ॥ ९४॥

these are external, whereas the Self is inward. How could Jaimini prompt it?

Text 309

1 · 309

किंच ।

Further:

Text 310

1 · 310

न तावद्योग एवास्ति शरीरेणात्मनः सदा ।

The Self is never connected with the body;

Verse 1.95

1.95

विषयैर्दूरतो नास्ति स्वर्गादौ स्यात्कथं सुखम् ॥ ९५॥

how, then, could there be pleasure in heaven, which is far removed from sense-objects?

Text 312

1 · 312

यस्मादन्यथा नोपपद्यते ।

Since that would make no sense otherwise,

Text 313

1 · 313

नराभिमानिनं तस्मात्कारकाद्यात्मदर्शिनम् ।

the mantra speaks of a person who identifies as human and sees himself as an agent and the like;

Verse 1.96

1.96

मन्त्र आहोररीकृत्य "कुर्वन्" इति न निर्द्वयम् ॥ ९६॥

accepting that statement about action, it does not speak of the non-dual Self.

Text 315

1 · 315

यच्चोक्तं "विरहय्य " इति तदपि न सम्यगेव । तथापि तु न या काचित्क्रिया यत्र क्व चाध्याहरणीया किं तु या यत्राभिप्रेतसंबन्धं घटयितुं शक्नोत्याकाङ्क्षां च वाक्यस्य पूरयति सैवाध्याहरणीया । एवंविशिष्टा च क्रियास्माभिरभ्युपगतैव । सा तूपादित्सितवाक्यार्था विरोधिन्येव नाभूतार्थप्रादुर्भावफलेति ।

What you said—“sentences do not combine without an action-word”—is not quite correct. Not just any action-word may be supplied wherever one pleases. Only the verb that can establish the intended relation and complete the sentence’s expectation may be supplied, and we accept that. But in sentences whose intended meaning is knowledge, such a verb would conflict with their purpose, which is not to produce something that did not exist.

Text 316

1 · 316

षड्भावविकाररहितात्मवस्तुनो निर्धूताशेषद्वैतानर्थस्यापराधीनप्रकाशस्य विजिज्ञापयिषितत्वादस्यस्मीत्यादिक्रियापदं स्वमहिमसिद्धार्थप्रतिपादनसमर्थमभ्युपगन्तव्यं न विपरीतार्थप्रतिपादनपरमिति ।

Since the Self is free from the six modifications, free from every ill born of duality, self-luminous, and independent, the verb “am” in a sentence such as “I am” must be understood as conveying the reality already established in its own glory, not as conveying a contrary meaning.

Text 317

1 · 317

धावेदिति न दानार्थे पदं यद्वत्प्रयुज्यते ।

Just as the word “may run” is not used in the sense “may give,”

Verse 1.97

1.97

एधीत्यादि तथा नेच्छेत्स्वतः सिद्धार्थवाचिनि ॥ ९७॥

one should not take “be” and similar words as expressing a desire for what is already self-established.

Text 319

1 · 319

न च यथोक्तवस्तुवृत्तप्रतिपादनव्यतिरेकेण तत्त्वमस्यादिवाक्यं वाक्यार्थान्तरं वक्तीति शक्यमध्यवसातुमित्याह ।

Nor can it be concluded that sentences such as “That thou art” express a meaning other than the reality of things as stated. He says:

Text 320

1 · 320

तत्त्वमस्यादिवाक्यानां स्वतस्सिद्धार्थबोधनात् ।

Because such sentences reveal a meaning that is self-established,

Verse 1.98

1.98

अर्थान्तरं न संद्रष्टुं शक्यते त्रिदशैरपि ॥ ९८॥

not even the gods can discover another meaning in them.

Text 322

1 · 322

यस्मादेवम् ।

Since this is so,

Text 323

1 · 323

अतः सर्वाश्रमाणां तु वाङ्मनःकायकर्मभिः ।

for people in every stage of life, the actions of speech, mind, and body,

Verse 1.99

1.99

स्वनुष्ठितैर्न मुक्तिः स्याज्ज्ञानादेव हि सा यतः ॥ ९९॥

even when performed properly, do not bring liberation, which comes from knowledge alone.

Text 325

1 · 325

तस्माच्च कारणादेतदप्युपपन्नम् ।

For this reason, the following too is well said:

Text 326

1 · 326

स्वमनोरथसंक्लृप्तप्रज्ञाध्मातधियामतः ।

Therefore these words belong to those whose minds are inflated by their own imagined opinions;

Text 327

1 · 327

श्रोत्रियेष्वेव वाचस्ताः शोभन्ते नात्मवेदिषु ॥ १००।

they suit ritualist scholars, but not those who know the Self.

Chapter colophon

Adhyāya 1

इति प्रथमोऽध्यायः ॥ १॥

Thus ends the first chapter.

Adhyāya 2

Chapter heading

Adhyāya 2

अथ द्वितीयोऽध्यायः ।

Now begins the second chapter.

Text 2

2 · 2

प्रत्यक्षादीनामनेवंविषयत्वात्तेषां स्वारम्भकविषयोपनिपातित्वादात्मनश्चाशेषप्रमेयवैलक्षण्यात्सर्वानर्थैकहेत्वज्ञानापनोदिज्ञानदिवाकरोदयहेतुत्वं वस्तुमात्रयाथात्म्यप्रकाशनपटीयसस्तत्त्वमस्यादेर्वचस एवेति बह्वीभिरुपपत्तिभिः प्रदर्शितम् ।

Perception and the other ordinary means of knowledge do not take the Self as their object; they operate only on the objects that give rise to them. The Self is unlike every knowable object. Therefore only words such as “That thou art,” which clearly reveal things as they are, bring about the rising of the sun of knowledge that removes ignorance, the cause of every ill. This has been shown through many arguments.

Text 3

2 · 3

अतस्तदर्थारतिपत्तौ यत्कारणं तदपनयनाय द्वितीयोऽध्याय आरभ्यते ।

The second chapter now begins to remove the obstacle that prevents understanding the meaning of that sentence.

Text 4

2 · 4

श्रावितो वेत्ति वाक्यार्थं नचेत्तत्त्वमसीत्यतः ।

If a listener does not understand the meaning of the sentence “That thou art,”

Verse 2.1

2.1

त्वंपदार्थानभिज्ञत्वादतस्तत्प्रक्रियोच्यते ॥ १॥

it is because the meaning of “thou” is not understood; that meaning is therefore explained.

Text 6

2 · 6

योऽयमहंब्रह्मेति वाक्यार्थस्तत्प्रतिपत्तिर्वाक्यादेवेति प्रत्यक्षादीनामनेवंविषयत्वादित्यवादिषं तस्य विशुद्ध्यर्थमनैकात्त्रिकत्वं पूर्वपक्षत्वेनोपस्थाप्यते ।

I said that the meaning of the sentence “I am Brahman” is known from the sentence alone, since perception and the other means of knowledge cannot know it. To clarify that point, the opponent’s claim that the reasoning is inconclusive is introduced:

Text 7

2 · 7

कृत्स्नानात्मनिवृत्तौ च कश्चिदाप्नोति निर्वृतिम् ।

One person gains peace by excluding the whole not-Self;

Verse 2.2

2.2

श्रुतवाक्यस्मृतेश्चान्यः स्मार्यते च वचोऽपरः ॥ २॥

another recalls it from a sentence he has heard, while yet another is reminded by another person’s words.

Text 9

2 · 9

एतत्प्रसङ्गेन श्रोत्रन्तरोपन्यासमुभयत्रापि संभावनायाह ।

To show that another listener is possible in either case, he says:

Text 10

2 · 10

वाक्यश्रवणमात्राच्च पिशाचकवदाप्नुयात् ।

One might attain the meaning from hearing a sentence alone, like a spirit;

Verse 2.3

2.3

त्रिषु यादृच्छिकी सिद्धिः स्मार्यमाणे तु निश्चिता ॥ ३॥

among these three, success may be accidental, while in the one who is reminded it is certain.

Text 12

2 · 12

नायमनैकान्तिको हेतुः । यतः ।

This reason is not inconclusive, because:

Text 13

2 · 13

सर्वोऽयं महिमा ज्ञेयो वाक्यस्यैव यथोदितः ।

All this power belongs to the sentence alone, exactly as has been stated;

Verse 2.4

2.4

वाक्यार्थं न ह्यृते वाक्यात्कश्चिज्जानाति तत्त्वतः ॥ ४॥

no one truly knows the meaning of a sentence apart from the sentence.

Text 15

2 · 15

वाक्यं च प्रतिपादनाय प्रवृत्तं सत्प्रतिपादयत्येव सर्वप्रमाणानामप्येवंवृत्तत्वात् ।

A sentence, when used to explain, does indeed explain an existing thing; all means of knowledge function in this way.

Text 16

2 · 16

नाहंग्राह्ये न तद्धीने न प्रत्यङ्नापि दुःखिनि ।

“Not grasped as ‘I,’ not dependent on that [ego], not inward, nor subject to suffering”—

Verse 2.5

2.5

विरोधः सदसीत्यस्माद्वाक्याभिज्ञस्य जायते ॥ ५॥

for one who understands sentences, there is no contradiction in the statement “That thou art.”

Text 18

2 · 18

नाविरक्तस्य संसारान्निविवृत्सा ततो भवेत् ।

One who is not dispassionate toward saṃsāra does not develop a desire to withdraw from it;

Verse 2.6

2.6

न चानिवृत्ततृष्णस्य पुरुषस्य मुमुक्षुता ॥ ६॥

nor does a person whose thirst for objects remains become a seeker of liberation.

Text 20

2 · 20

न चामुमुक्षोरस्तीह गुरुपादोपसर्पणम् ।

One who does not seek liberation does not approach the feet of a teacher;

Verse 2.7

2.7

न विना गुरुसंबन्धं वाक्यस्य श्रवणं भवेत् ॥ ७॥

without a relationship with a teacher, the sentence is not heard.

Text 22

2 · 22

तथा पदपदाथौ च न स्तो वाक्यमृते क्वचित् ।

Likewise, words and their meanings do not stand apart from a sentence;

Verse 2.8

2.8

अन्वयव्यतिरेकौ च तावृते स्तां कैमाश्रयौ ॥ ८॥

and without them, what could support agreement and exclusion?

Text 24

2 · 24

अन्वयव्यतिरेकाभ्यां विना वाक्यार्थबोधनम् ।

Without agreement and exclusion, the meaning of a sentence cannot be understood;

Verse 2.9

2.9

न स्यात्तेन विनाज्ञानप्रहाणं नोपपद्यते ॥ ९॥

without that understanding, the removal of ignorance cannot occur.

Text 26

2 · 26

विनाज्ञानप्रहाणेन पुरुषार्थः सुदुर्लभः ।

Without the removal of ignorance, the human end is exceedingly hard to attain;

Verse 2.10

2.10

तस्माद्यथोक्तसिद्ध्यर्थं परो ग्रन्थोऽवतार्यते ॥ १०॥

therefore the following text is introduced to establish the realization just described.

Text 28

2 · 28

वर्चस्कं त्वन्नकार्यत्वाद्यथा नात्मेति गम्यते ।

Excrement is known to be not-Self because it is produced from food;

Verse 2.11

2.11

तद्भागः सेन्द्रियो देहस्तद्वत्किमिति नेक्ष्यते ॥ ११॥

why is the body, with its sense-organs, not seen in the same way as one part of that food?

Text 30

2 · 30

आद्यन्तयोरनात्मत्वे प्रसिद्धे मध्येऽपि कः प्रतिबन्धः ।

When the beginning and end are known to be not-Self, what prevents the middle from being seen as not-Self too?

Text 31

2 · 31

प्रागनात्मैव जग्धं सदात्मतामेत्यविद्यया ।

The world, which was not-Self from the beginning, is taken by ignorance to be the Self;

Verse 2.12

2.12

स्रगालेपनवद्देहं तस्मात्पश्येद्विविक्तधीः ॥ १२॥

therefore the discerning person should see the body as distinct, like a garland or ointment.

Text 33

2 · 33

अथैवमपि मद्वचनं नाद्रियसे स्वयमेवैतस्माच्छरीराद् अशुचिराशेर्निराशो भविष्यसि ।

Even if you do not heed my words, you will become disenchanted with this body yourself, since it is a heap of impurities.

Text 34

2 · 34

मन्यसे तावदस्मीति यावदस्मान्न नीयसे ।

You think, “I am this,” only so long as you have not been carried away from here;

Verse 2.13

2.13

श्वभिः क्रोडीकृते देहे नैवं त्वमभिमंस्यसे ॥ १३॥

when dogs have seized the body, you will no longer think, “I am this.”

Text 36

2 · 36

शिर आक्रम्य पादेन भर्त्सयत्यपरान् शुनः ।

One dog steps on its head and drives the other dogs away;

Verse 2.14

2.14

दृष्ट्वा साधारणं देहं कस्मात्सक्तोऽसि तत्र भोः ॥ १४॥

seeing that the body is common to all, why are you attached to it, friend?

Text 38

2 · 38

श्रुतिपरिप्रापितोऽयमर्थोऽनात्मा बुद्ध्यादिदेहान्त इतीदमाह ।

The point delivered by scripture—that from the intellect down to the body all is not-Self—is stated:

Text 39

2 · 39

बुसव्रीहिपलालांशैर्बीजमेकं त्रिधा यथा ।

Just as one seed is divided into three parts: husk, grain, and chaff,

Verse 2.15

2.15

बुद्धिमांसपुरीषांशैरन्नं तद्वदवस्थितम् ॥ १५॥

so food is divided into intellect, flesh, and excrement.

Text 41

2 · 41

यथोक्तार्थप्रतिपत्तौ सत्यां न रागद्वेषाभ्यां विक्रियते विपश्चिदित्यस्यार्थस्य प्रतिपत्तये दृष्टान्तः ।

An example is given to show that once this point is understood, the discerning person is not altered by attachment or aversion:

Text 42

2 · 42

वर्चस्के संपरित्यक्ते दोषतश्चावधारिते ।

When excrement has been discarded and recognized as impure,

Verse 2.16

2.16

यदि दोषं वदेत्तस्मै किं तत्रोच्चरितुर्भवेत् ॥ १६॥

what fault could there be for the person who spoke of its impurity?

Text 44

2 · 44

तद्वत्सूक्ष्मे तथा स्थूले देहे त्यक्ते विवेकतः ।

Likewise, when the subtle and gross bodies have been discarded through discernment,

Verse 2.17

2.17

यदि दोषं वदेत्ताभ्यां किं तत्र विदुषो भवेत् ॥ १७॥

what fault in them could affect the knower?

Text 46

2 · 46

एतावदेव ह्यहं ब्रह्मास्मीति वाक्यार्थाप्रतिपत्तौ कारणं यदुत बुद्ध्यादौ देहान्ते ह्यहंममेति निस्सन्धिबन्धनो ग्रहः ।

The only obstacle to understanding the sentence “I am Brahman” is the unbroken grasp “I” and “mine” in the intellect and the rest, extending to the body.

Text 47

2 · 47

तद्व्यतिरेके हि न कुतश्चिद्विभज्यत एकल एव प्रत्यगात्मन्यवतिष्ठत इत्याह ।

When one is separated from all that, one remains alone in the inward Self, indivisible from anything else. This is stated:

Text 48

2 · 48

रिपौ बन्धौ स्वदेहे च समैकात्म्यं प्रपश्यतः ।

For one who sees equal oneness in an enemy, a friend, and one’s own body,

Verse 2.18

2.18

विवेकिनः कुतः कोपः स्वदेहावयवेश्विव ॥ १८॥

where could anger arise in the discerning person, any more than toward the limbs of one’s own body?

Text 50

2 · 50

इतश्चानात्मा देहादिः ।

The body and the rest are not-Self for another reason too:

Text 51

2 · 51

घटादिवच्च दृश्यत्वात्तैरेव करणैर्दृशेः ।

they are seen, like a pot, by the very instruments that reveal them;

Verse 2.19

2.19

स्वप्ने चानन्वयाज्ज्ञयो देहोऽनात्मेति सूरिभिः ॥ १९॥

and because they are not continuous with the Self in a dream, the wise know that the body is not-Self.

Text 53

2 · 53

देहादिकार्यकरणसंघातव्यतिरेकाव्यतिरेकदर्शिनः प्रत्यक्षत एव विरुद्धं कार्यमुपलभ्यते ।

Those who examine whether the body, its effects, and its instruments are connected or disconnected directly observe effects that contradict one another.

Text 54

2 · 54

चतुर्भिरुह्यते यत्तत्सर्वशक्त्या शरीरकम् ।

The body is supported by the four elements through all their power;

Verse 2.20

2.20

तूलायते तदेवाहंधियाघ्रातमचेतसाम् ॥ २०॥

ignorant people, however, take that very body—light as cotton—to be “I,” through the thought of ego.

Text 56

2 · 56

प्रसिद्धत्वाप्रकरणार्थोपसंहारायाह ।

To conclude the subject, which is already well known, he says:

Text 57

2 · 57

स्थूलं युक्त्या निरस्यैवं नभसो नीलतामिव ।

Having reasoned away the gross body, as one reasons away the blueness of the sky,

Verse 2.21

2.21

देहं सूक्ष्मं निराकुर्यादतो युक्तिभिरात्मनः ॥ २१॥

one should now reject the subtle body as the Self, using these arguments.

Text 59

2 · 59

कथं देहं सूक्ष्मं निराकृर्यादिति । उच्यते ।

How is the subtle body rejected? It is explained:

Text 60

2 · 60

अहंममत्वयत्नेच्छा नात्मधर्माः कृशत्ववत् ।

“I”-ness, “mine”-ness, effort, and desire are not properties of the Self, any more than leanness is;

Verse 2.22

2.22

कर्मत्वेनोपलभ्यत्वादपायित्वाच्च वस्त्रवत् ॥ २२॥

they are objects of awareness and are impermanent, like clothing.

Text 62

2 · 62

वैधर्म्ये दृष्टान्तः ।

An example of difference is given:

Text 63

2 · 63

नोष्णिमानं दहत्यग्निः स्वरूपत्वाद्यथा ज्वलन् ।

While it burns, fire does not burn its own heat, since heat is its very nature;

Verse 2.23

2.23

तथैवात्मात्मनो विद्यादहं नैवाविशेषतः ॥ २३॥

likewise, the Self should be known as non-different from its own awareness, not as something distinct.

Text 65

2 · 65

एकस्यात्मनः कर्मकर्तृभावः सर्वथा नोपपद्यत इति श्रुत्वा मीमांसकः प्रत्यवतिष्ठते । अहंप्रत्ययग्राह्यत्वाद्ग्राहक आत्मेति तन्निवृत्त्यर्थमाह ।

After hearing that one and the same Self cannot be both agent and object of action, the Mīmāṃsaka objects: “The Self is the knower because it is grasped by the cognition ‘I.’” To remove this view, he says:

Text 66

2 · 66

यत्कर्मको हि यो भावो नासौ तत्कर्तृको यतः ।

Whatever is the object of an action is not the agent of that action;

Verse 2.24

2.24

घटप्रत्ययवत्तस्मान्नाहं स्याद्द्रष्टृकर्मकः ॥ २४॥

therefore the “I” cannot be the seer’s object, just as a pot is the object of the cognition “pot.”

Text 68

2 · 68

अत्राह प्रत्यक्षेणात्मनः कर्मकर्तृत्वाभ्युपगमेतत्पादोपजीविनानुमानेन प्रत्यक्षोत्सारणमयुक्तमिति चोद्यं तन्निराकरणाय प्रत्यक्षोपन्यासः ।

An objection is raised: “Direct perception accepts that the Self is both agent and object. It is wrong to use an inference that depends on perception to set perception aside.” Direct perception is now cited to answer it.

Text 69

2 · 69

यत्र यो दृश्यते द्रष्ट्रा तस्यैवासौ गुणो न तु ।

Whatever is seen by a seer has its qualities in itself, not in the seer;

Verse 2.25

2.25

द्रष्टृस्थं दृश्यतां यस्मान्नैवेयाद्द्रष्टृबोधवत् ॥ २५॥

for what belongs to the seer would not become an object of sight, just as the awareness of the seer does not.

Text 71

2 · 71

प्रत्यक्षेणैव भवदभिमतस्य प्रत्यक्षस्याभासीकृतत्वात् सुस्थमेवानुमानम् । अतस्तदेव प्रक्रियते तत्र च विकल्पदूषणाभिधानम् ।

Since direct perception itself has shown that your supposed perception is illusory, the inference stands firm. He now states the inference and its alternatives.

Text 72

2 · 72

नात्मना न तदंशेन गुणः स्वस्थोऽवगम्यते ।

A quality such as awareness is not known to rest in the Self, either as the Self or as one of its parts,

Verse 2.26

2.26

अभिन्नत्वात्समत्वाच्च निरंशत्वादकर्मतः ॥ २६॥

because the Self is non-different, equal, without parts, and not an agent.

Text 74

2 · 74

न युगपन्नपि क्रमेणोभयथा चैकस्य धर्मिणो ग्राह्यग्राहकत्वमुपपद्यत इति प्रतिपादनायाह ।

To show that the same substance cannot be both the knower and the known, either simultaneously or in sequence, he says:

Text 75

2 · 75
